infusions, hemodynamic monitoring, and prolonged therapy, but may cause bloodstream
infection, thrombosis, or mechanical complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Central Venous Pressure} Pressure measured in or near the right atrium, usually through a central venous catheter. It reflects
right-sided filling conditions but is affected by ventilation, heart function, vascular tone,
and measurement technique; it should not be used alone to guide fluid treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Centriacinar Emphysema} A subtype of emphysema in which destruction begins in the respiratory bronchioles and
extends peripherally. It predominantly affects the upper lobes and is strongly
associated with cigarette smoking. It is the most common form of emphysema and often
coexists with chronic bronchitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Centrifuge} Spins samples to separate components by density. Used for blood processing, urine
analysis, tissue preparation. Speed: RPM or RCF.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cephalalgia} Headache or pain in the head, including pain arising from cranial or related structures.
It is a symptom with many possible primary and secondary causes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cephalhaematoma} A collection of blood between the periosteum and the skull bone, caused by rupture of
blood vessels during childbirth, most commonly associated with ventouse (vacuum)
delivery. Unlike caput succedaneum, a cephalhaematoma does not cross suture lines and is
limited by the periosteal attachments.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cephalohematoma} A collection of blood beneath the periosteum of a newborn's skull, usually caused by
birth trauma. It is confined by suture lines and generally resolves gradually.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cephalopelvic Disproportion} A mismatch between the size or shape of the maternal pelvis and the fetal head that
prevents or impedes vaginal delivery. It may be suspected from clinical assessment but
cannot always be diagnosed reliably before labor.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cephalosporin} A class of beta-lactam antibiotics derived from the fungus Cephalosporium acremonium,
with a mechanism identical to penicillins — inhibition of bacterial cell wall synthesis
by binding to penicillin-binding proteins and inhibiting transpeptidase. Cephalosporins
are classified into five generations based on their antimicrobial spectrum.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Ceramide} A sphingolipid composed of a sphingoid base linked to a fatty acid. Ceramides are
structural components of cell membranes and the epidermal permeability barrier and also
participate in cell signaling.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerclage} A surgical placement of a suture around the cervix to prevent preterm delivery in
patients with cervical insufficiency. Types include transvaginal (McDonald or Shirodkar)
and transabdominal. Usually placed at 12-14 weeks gestation and removed at 36-37 weeks.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebellar Ataxia} A neurological sign characterized by impaired coordination of voluntary movements,
resulting from dysfunction of the cerebellum or its connections. The cerebellum
coordinates the timing, force, and accuracy of movements, and cerebellar dysfunction
produces a characteristic constellation of findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebellar tumor} A neoplasm involving the cerebellum that may cause ataxia, dysmetria, nystagmus,
vomiting, or hydrocephalus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebellitis} Inflammation of the cerebellum, often after infection and sometimes associated with an
autoimmune response. It may produce ataxia, dysarthria, nystagmus, headache, and altered
consciousness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebellopontine Angle} The angle-shaped region at the junction of the cerebellum, pons, and petrous temporal
bone. Lesions in this space may affect cranial nerves and commonly include vestibular
schwannoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebellopontine Angle Tumor} Lesion at the cerebellopontine angle (CPA), an anatomic space between the cerebellum and
pons, medial to the temporal lobe and lateral to the brainstem, containing CN V
(trigeminal), VII (facial), VIII (vestibulocochlear), and the anterior inferior
cerebellar artery (AICA).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebellum} Largest brain portion (approximately 10 percent of volume but over 50 percent of
neurons) in the posterior cranial fossa behind the brainstem. Two hemispheres and a
vermis, divided into vestibulocerebellum (balance, eye movements), spinocerebellum
(posture, gait, coordination), and cerebrocerebellum (motor planning, cognition).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Amyloid Angiopathy} Deposition of amyloid beta in the walls of small and medium cerebral and leptomeningeal
arteries. It commonly causes recurrent lobar intracerebral hemorrhage in older adults
and may also cause transient focal neurological episodes or cognitive decline.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Aneurysm} An abnormal focal enlargement of an artery in or around the brain. It may remain
asymptomatic or rupture and cause subarachnoid hemorrhage. Risk factors include hypertension, smoking, certain inherited
disorders, and family history.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Angiography} Contrast imaging of cerebral vessels. Evaluates aneurysms, AVMs, stenosis. Invasive
procedure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Arteriovenous Malformation} An abnormal direct connection between cerebral arteries and veins without a normal
capillary bed. It may cause intracranial hemorrhage, seizures, headache, or focal
neurological deficits and is evaluated with brain imaging and angiography.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Autoregulation} The intrinsic ability of cerebral arterioles to maintain relatively stable cerebral
blood flow across a range of perfusion pressures. It may be impaired by severe brain
injury, ischemia, hypercapnia, or anesthetic and vasoactive drugs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral cavernous malformations} Low-flow vascular malformations composed of dilated, thin-walled capillaries without
intervening brain parenchyma, often multiple and familial (autosomal dominant with
mutations in CCM1, CCM2, or CCM3 genes). They may present with seizures, focal
neurological deficits, or intracerebral hemorrhage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Cortex} The outermost layer of the cerebrum, composed of gray matter (neuronal cell bodies)
folded into gyri (ridges) and sulci (grooves), greatly increasing surface area.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Edema} The accumulation of excess fluid within the brain parenchyma, leading to increased
intracranial pressure and potentially fatal brain herniation. There are three main types
with different pathophysiological mechanisms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Hemorrhage} Bleeding within the brain parenchyma, a type of hemorrhagic stroke causing mass effect,
edema, and neurological deficits. Causes: hypertension (most common—basal ganglia,
thalamus), cerebral amyloid angiopathy (lobar, elderly), arteriovenous malformation,
aneurysm, anticoagulants, trauma, tumors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Hypoxia} Insufficient oxygen delivery to the brain, caused by cardiac arrest, respiratory
failure, severe hypotension, or another interruption of oxygen supply. Prolonged hypoxia
can produce permanent neuronal injury and hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Infarction} Cerebral infarction, or ischemic stroke, results from occlusion of a cerebral artery
causing irreversible neuronal damage. The most common cause is thromboembolism from
atherosclerotic carotid or intracranial arteries, or cardioembolism from atrial
fibrillation or cardiac thrombus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Lobes} Four lobes divided by sulci. Frontal (anterior to central sulcus): executive function,
motor control (precentral gyrus), Broca's area. Parietal (between central and
parieto-occipital sulci): somatosensory (postcentral gyrus), spatial awareness. Temporal
(below lateral sulcus): auditory processing, memory (hippocampus), Wernicke's area.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Palsy} A group of permanent disorders of movement and posture development causing activity
limitation, attributed to non-progressive disturbances occurring in the developing fetal
or infant brain. It is the most common motor disability in childhood, affecting
approximately 1 in 500 live births.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Peduncle} Paired structures of the ventral midbrain carrying descending motor tracts
(corticospinal, corticobulbar, corticopontine) connecting the cerebrum to the pons and
below. Lesions produce contralateral hemiparesis; Weber syndrome (peduncle + CN III)
causes ipsilateral CN III palsy with contralateral hemiparesis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Perfusion Pressure} The pressure gradient driving blood flow through the brain, approximated as mean
arterial pressure minus intracranial pressure. CPP falls when systemic pressure
decreases or ICP rises and must be interpreted with the clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Salt Wasting} Renal sodium wasting causing hyponatremia and volume depletion occurring after
intracranial surgery or in setting of intracranial disease. Distinguished from SIADH by
the presence of volume depletion with negative sodium balance and compensatory elevation
of renin and aldosterone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral tumor} A neoplasm arising within or involving the brain, producing symptoms according to its
location, growth rate, and effect on intracranial pressure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Venous Sinus Thrombosis} Thrombosis of the cerebral venous sinuses (most commonly the superior sagittal sinus;
also transverse, sigmoid, straight; or cortical veins), with resultant venous
hypertension, intracranial hypertension, hemorrhagic cortical infarcts, and venous
congestion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebral Visual Impairment} Visual impairment caused by damage to the brain's visual pathways or occipital cortex
rather than the eye itself; the leading cause of childhood visual impairment in
developed countries. Features include poor visual attention, difficulty recognizing
faces or objects, and variable vision; management is educational and rehabilitative.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebriform nevus sebaceous} A variant of nevus sebaceous presenting as a soft, cerebriform (brain-like),
pedunculated or sessile nodule, often found on the scalp or face. It is a benign adnexal
hamartoma composed of sebaceous glands, but like other nevus sebaceous lesions, it
carries a low risk of secondary neoplasms and may warrant monitoring or excision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebritis} Inflammatory process in brain parenchyma without abscess formation. May precede abscess.
Causes: bacterial, fungal, parasitic infections. Diagnosis: MRI with contrast showing
enhancement. Treatment: antibiotics/antifungals, surgical drainage if abscess forms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebroplacental Ratio} The ratio of the middle cerebral artery (MCA) pulsatility index to the umbilical artery
(UA) pulsatility index. CPR <5th percentile (or <1.0-1.1) suggests fetal brain-sparing
effect (vasodilation of cerebral vessels in response to hypoxemia) due to placental
insufficiency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebrospinal Fluid} A clear, colorless fluid that fills the ventricles of the brain, the central canal of
the spinal cord, and the subarachnoid space around the brain and spinal cord. Produced
primarily by the choroid plexus of the lateral, third, and fourth ventricles (~500
mL\/day, total volume ~150 mL in adults).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebrospinal Fluid Analysis} Laboratory examination of cerebrospinal fluid, usually obtained by lumbar puncture. It may
assess infection, bleeding, inflammation, demyelinating disease, or malignant cells.
Analysis can include opening pressure, appearance, cell counts, protein, glucose,
microbiology, and other tests selected for the clinical question.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebrovascular Accident} An acute neurological deficit caused by interruption of blood flow to the brain or bleeding
within the brain. See Stroke.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerebrovascular Accident (Stroke)} Sudden brain dysfunction caused by interrupted blood flow or bleeding in the brain. See Stroke.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Stroke

\medterm{Cerebrum} The largest part of the brain, comprising two cerebral hemispheres connected by the
corpus callosum. Each hemisphere has four lobes: frontal (executive function, motor,
Broca's area), parietal (somatosensory, spatial awareness), temporal (auditory, memory,
Wernicke's area), occipital (visual processing).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Ceruloplasmin} The principal copper-carrying protein in blood, synthesized mainly by the liver. Its
concentration and copper-binding function are clinically relevant in disorders such as
Wilson disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerumen} A substance that helps keep dirt out of the ear and lubricates the skin in the ear. More
commonly known as earwax.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cerumen Impaction} Accumulation of earwax in the external auditory canal that causes symptoms or prevents
visualization of the tympanic membrane. It may cause aural fullness, conductive hearing
loss, tinnitus, itching, or pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Cancer} A malignant tumour arising from the cervix, most commonly caused by persistent infection
with high-risk human papillomavirus. It may be asymptomatic early or cause abnormal
vaginal bleeding, discharge, or pelvic pain; prevention and early detection rely on HPV
vaccination, screening, and assessment of abnormal results.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical carcinoma} A malignant tumor of the uterine cervix, most commonly associated with persistent
high-risk human papillomavirus infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Cerclage} Surgical placement of a non-absorbable suture around the cervix to provide mechanical
support and prevent preterm delivery in patients with cervical insufficiency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Dilatation} Progressive opening of the cervical os during labor, measured in centimeters from 0
(closed) to 10 cm (fully dilated). The cervix must also efface (thin out) before
significant dilatation occurs. Cervical status assessed by digital vaginal examination
during labor.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Dilation} The opening of the cervical canal during labor, measured in centimeters from closed (0
cm) to fully dilated (10 cm); required for vaginal delivery. Occurs with effacement
(thinning/shortening of the cervix) during the first stage of labor, driven by effective
uterine contractions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Disc Herniation} A common cause of neck pain and radiculopathy, occurring when the nucleus pulposus of an
intervertebral disc in the cervical spine herniates through the annulus fibrosus,
compressing adjacent nerve roots or the spinal cord.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Dysplasia} Abnormal growth of cervical epithelial cells, representing a premalignant condition that
may progress to cervical cancer if untreated. Also known as cervical intraepithelial
neoplasia (CIN). CIN is caused by persistent infection with high-risk human
papillomavirus (HPV) types, most commonly HPV 16 and 18.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
cervical intraepithelial.

\medterm{Cervical Dystonia} A focal movement disorder causing involuntary contractions of neck muscles and abnormal
positioning of the head. It may produce pain, tremor, and sustained or intermittent
twisting of the neck.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Effacement} Thinning and shortening of the cervix during labor, measured as a percentage from 0\%
(thick, normal length) to 100\% (paper thin). Effacement precedes and accompanies
cervical dilatation. Nulliparous women often efface before dilating; multiparous women
may efface and dilate simultaneously.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Exam in Labor} A digital vaginal examination used to assess cervical dilation, effacement, consistency,
and position, together with the station and position of the fetal head. Serial examinations help assess labor progress.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Incompetence} The inability of the cervix to maintain pregnancy in the second trimester due to
painless cervical dilatation. Results in recurrent second trimester pregnancy loss or
preterm delivery. Risk factors include prior cervical trauma, cone biopsy, and
congenital anomalies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Length Screening} Transvaginal ultrasound measurement of the cervix between 18-24 weeks to assess risk of
spontaneous preterm birth. Normal cervix measures greater than 25 mm. Short cervix (less
than 25 mm) increases preterm delivery risk. Cervical funneling and dilatation are
additional concerning findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Mucus} Secretions from the cervical glands that change throughout the menstrual cycle under
hormonal influence. Estrogen produces thin, clear, stretchy mucus favorable for sperm
transport around ovulation (spinnbarkeit). Progesterone produces thick, sticky, opaque
mucus that forms a plug in the cervical canal during pregnancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Polyps} Usually benign, finger-like growths arising from the endocervical canal or cervix. They
may cause irregular bleeding, postcoital bleeding, or vaginal discharge and are often
found during a pelvic examination. Symptomatic or atypical polyps are removed and sent
for pathology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Pregnancy} A rare ectopic pregnancy (0.1-1\%) implanted in the cervical canal. Presents with
painless vaginal bleeding in the first trimester. Diagnosis: ultrasound
(hourglass-shaped uterus, gestational sac in the cervix with absent sliding sign, empty
uterine cavity). High risk of severe hemorrhage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Radiculopathy} A condition caused by compression, irritation, or inflammation of a spinal nerve root in
the cervical spine, resulting in pain, numbness, tingling, and weakness in the
distribution of the affected nerve root.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Rib} An extra rib arising from a cervical vertebra, usually the seventh. It may be
asymptomatic or contribute to thoracic outlet syndrome by compressing the brachial
plexus or subclavian vessels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Ripening Agents} Medicines or mechanical devices used to soften and prepare the cervix before labor
induction. Options include prostaglandins and cervical balloons; selection and dosing depend on the cervix, pregnancy,
contraindications, and local obstetric protocol.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Screening (Pap Smear)} Screening for cervical cancer by collecting cells from the transformation zone of the
cervix. Liquid-based cytology (ThinPrep) or conventional Pap smear. HPV co-testing
(high-risk HPV 16, 18, others) improves sensitivity.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Pap Smear

\medterm{Cervical Spine} The upper segment of the vertebral column consisting of seven cervical vertebrae, C1
through C7, supporting the head and protecting the cervical spinal cord. It permits
substantial flexion, extension, rotation, and lateral flexion while transmitting the
vertebral arteries through the transverse foramina of C1-C6.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Spine Trauma} Injury to the cervical spine that may affect the vertebrae, discs, ligaments, spinal cord,
or nerve roots. Evaluation includes the mechanism, examination, and appropriate imaging;
classification systems can help guide treatment, which depends on stability and
neurologic findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical spondyloarthropathy} A degenerative condition of the cervical spine involving disc degeneration, osteophyte
formation, and facet joint arthropathy that may compress nerve roots or the spinal cord.
It can cause neck pain, radiculopathy, or myelopathy, and is a common cause of cervical
spinal stenosis in older adults.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Spondylosis} Age-related degenerative changes of the cervical spine—disc degeneration, osteophyte
formation, facet joint arthritis, and ligament hypertrophy—affecting the intervertebral
discs, vertebrae, and joints.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervical Vertebrae} The seven vertebrae of the neck. Typical cervical vertebrae have small bodies, bifid
spinous processes, and transverse foramina transmitting the vertebral vessels; C1, C2,
and C7 are specialized.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervicitis} Cervicitis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
infections (Chlamydia trachomatis and Neisseria gonorrhoeae) but also by other organisms
(Mycoplasma genitalium, Trichomonas vaginalis, herpes simplex virus), or non-infectious
causes (chemical irritation, foreign body, allergy).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cervix} Cervix concerns cervix, a body structure, substance, or physiologic concept whose clinical meaning depends on location, function, and patient context.
approximately 3-4 cm long in the non-pregnant state and consists of two parts: the
ectocervix (visible portion protruding into the vagina, covered by stratified squamous
epithelium) and the endocervical canal (lined by columnar epithelium).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cesarean Delivery} Surgical delivery of the fetus through abdominal and uterine incisions. Performed when
vaginal delivery is contraindicated or fails. Indications include malpresentation,
placenta previa, previous cesarean, fetal distress, and failed induction. Most common
uterine incision is low transverse (classical for very preterm or transverse lie).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cesarean Section} C-section: surgical delivery of the infant through incisions in the abdominal wall and
uterus. Indications: fetal distress, failure to progress, malpresentation (breech,
transverse), placenta previa, abruption, previous cesarean (some), multiple pregnancy,
preeclampsia, cord prolapse, and maternal conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cesarean Section (C-section)} Surgical delivery of a fetus through incisions in the abdominal wall (laparotomy) and
uterus (hysterotomy).



\textbf{Synonyms}:
C-section

\medterm{Cestodiasis} Infection or infestation caused by cestodes, commonly called tapeworms. Disease may
involve the intestine or tissues, depending on the species and life-cycle stage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
tapeworms.

\medterm{CFTR Modulator Therapy} Targeted therapies correcting the defective CFTR protein.
Elexacaftor-tezacaftor-ivacaftor (Trikafta) is the most effective combination for
patients with at least one F508del mutation. These therapies have dramatically changed
CF prognosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chagas Disease} A parasitic infection caused by Trypanosoma cruzi, transmitted by reduviid bugs (kissing
bugs), endemic in Latin America. Acute phase: fever, swelling at bite site (chagoma),
Romana sign (periorbital edema). Chronic phase (years later): Chagas cardiomyopathy
(most common and serious), megaesophagus, and megacolon.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chagoma} A localized inflammatory lesion at the site where Trypanosoma cruzi enters the body. It
is an early manifestation of Chagas disease and may be accompanied by regional
lymphadenopathy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chain of Custody} Meticulous paper trail and chronological documentation tracking the seizure, custody,
control, transfer, analysis, and disposition of physical and electronic evidence in
medico-legal investigations (e.g., blood samples for toxicology, bullets, sexual assault
kits, biological fluids).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chalazion} Chalazion is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chancre} A usually painless ulcer at the site of inoculation of an infection, especially the
primary lesion of syphilis. It may be accompanied by regional lymph-node enlargement and
heals without treatment, although infection persists.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chancroid} Chancroid is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Channelopathy} A disorder caused by abnormal structure or function of an ion channel or its regulatory
proteins. Channelopathies can affect excitable tissues and cause neurologic, muscular,
or cardiac disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Charcoal} Fine black powder produced by controlled pyrolysis of organic materials, processed to
yield a massive micro-porous surface area (500-1500 m2/g). See activated charcoal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Charcot Arthropathy} Progressive destruction and deformity of a joint caused by loss of protective pain or
proprioceptive sensation. It is most often associated with peripheral neuropathy,
particularly diabetic neuropathy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Charcot Foot} A progressive destructive arthropathy of the foot and ankle, usually occurring in people
with peripheral neuropathy, especially diabetes. Repeated unnoticed trauma causes
warmth, swelling, deformity, instability, and an increased risk of ulceration and
amputation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Charcot Joint} Progressive, neurotrophic arthropathy caused by loss of sensation (proprioception and
pain) leading to joint destruction with bone fragmentation, joint dislocation,
deformity, and loss of normal pain and proprioceptive sensation in the affected limb.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Charcot Triad} Clinical constellation of fever, right upper quadrant pain, and jaundice representing
the classic presentation of ascending cholangitis, infection of the biliary tree from
common bile duct obstruction by a gallstone. The full triad is found in approximately
fifty to seventy percent of patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Charcot Triad (Cholangitis)} Classic diagnostic triad of acute ascending cholangitis: (1) Fever, (2) Right Upper
Quadrant abdominal pain, and (3) Jaundice. Reynolds Pentad adds Hypotension and Altered
Mental Status. See Acute Cholangitis.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Cholangitis

\medterm{Charcot-Marie-Tooth Disease} Group of inherited peripheral neuropathies (hereditary motor and sensory neuropathy,
HMSN), the most common inherited neuromuscular disorder (prevalence 1 in 2,500). More
than 100 causative genes (PMP22, MPZ, GJB1, MFN2, NEFL).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CHARGE Syndrome} A genetic disorder caused by CHD7 gene mutations (autosomal dominant, most cases de
novo) affecting multiple organ systems.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Checkpoint Inhibitor} A medicine that blocks an inhibitory immune-checkpoint pathway, helping selected immune cells recognize and attack tumor cells. Examples target PD-1, PD-L1, or CTLA-4.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Checkpoints} Immune regulatory molecules. Cancer exploits checkpoints. Inhibitors block checkpoints.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chediak-Higashi Syndrome} Autosomal recessive (LYST mutation, lysosomal trafficking regulator). Giant lysosomal
granules in neutrophils, melanocytes, neurons. Partial oculocutaneous albinism, silvery
hair, photophobia, nystagmus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cheilectropion} An eversion deformity of the lip, usually involving the lower lip. It may result from
scarring, congenital malformation, or loss of tissue support.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cheilitis} Inflammation of one or both lips, causing dryness, scaling, fissuring, erythema,
swelling, pain, or ulceration. Causes include irritant or allergic contact exposure,
infection, nutritional deficiency, chronic sun exposure, and inflammatory skin disease;
persistent lesions require evaluation for premalignant or malignant change.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cheiloplasty} Plastic or reconstructive surgery of the lip, performed to repair congenital clefting,
trauma, tumors, scars, or other deformity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cheiloschisis} Cleft lip, a congenital fissure of the upper lip caused by incomplete fusion of facial
processes during embryonic development. It may occur alone or with cleft palate.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cheilosis} A disorder of the lips characterized by dryness, scaling, fissuring, or inflammation.
Causes include irritation, nutritional deficiency, infection, and systemic disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cheiropompholyx} A form of dyshidrotic eczema affecting the hands, with intensely itchy small vesicles on
the palms or sides of the fingers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chelation} The formation of a stable complex between a metal ion and a molecule with multiple
binding sites. In medicine, chelation can be used under supervision to bind and promote
elimination of selected toxic metals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chelation Study} Nuclear medicine study using chelating agents. Evaluates renal function, obstruction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chelation Therapy} Use of chelating agents to bind metals for excretion. Agents: EDTA (lead), deferoxamine
(iron), dimercaprol (arsenic, mercury, gold), DMSA (lead, mercury), penicillamine
(copper, lead). Complications: nephrotoxicity, electrolyte disturbances, redistribution
of metals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemical Eye Injury} Ocular damage caused by exposure to an acidic or alkaline substance. Immediate copious
irrigation, removal of particulate material, measurement of ocular-surface pH, and
urgent ophthalmic assessment are required; alkali injuries may penetrate deeply and
continue to worsen after exposure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemical Injury} Tissue damage from corrosive substances. Acid burns: coagulation necrosis, eschar
formation. Alkali burns: liquefaction necrosis, deeper penetration. Management: copious
irrigation, remove contaminated clothing, assess for airway involvement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemical Peels} Chemical peels use acids to exfoliate skin layers. Superficial peels (glycolic acid 20
to 70 percent, salicylic acid 20 to 30 percent) affect the epidermis. Medium peels
(trichloroacetic acid 35 to 50 percent) reach the papillary dermis. Deep peels (phenol
88 percent) reach the reticular dermis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemical Pneumonitis} Inflammation and lung injury caused by inhalation or aspiration of irritating chemicals,
gastric contents, hydrocarbons, or other toxic substances. It may cause cough, dyspnea,
hypoxemia, wheezing, and diffuse pulmonary infiltrates.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemodectoma} A tumor arising from paraganglionic tissue, especially a carotid-body tumor. It is
usually slow-growing and may compress nearby cranial nerves or vessels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemoembolization} A procedure that combines delivery of an antineoplastic agent with embolization of the
blood supply to a tumor, most commonly for selected liver cancers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemokine} A family of small secreted cytokines that direct leukocyte migration (chemotaxis) to
sites of infection, inflammation, and tissue repair. Classified by cysteine motif: CXC
(e.g., IL-8/CXCL8, recruit neutrophils), CC (e.g., MCP-1/CCL2, attract monocytes), C,
and CX3C.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemokinesis} Nonspecific stimulation of cell movement by a chemical substance, without directional
movement toward or away from a concentration gradient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemophobia} An excessive or irrational fear of chemicals, sometimes associated with anxiety or
obsessive concerns about contamination or toxicity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemoprevention} The use of drugs or natural agents to prevent, delay, or reverse cancer development,
e.g., tamoxifen or raloxifene in high-risk breast cancer, HPV and hepatitis B
vaccination, and aspirin in colorectal cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemoradiation} The concurrent combination of chemotherapy and radiation therapy, used to treat
locoregionally advanced cancers such as anal, cervical, head and neck, esophageal, and
limited-stage small cell lung cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemoreceptor} A sensory receptor that responds to changes in blood or CSF chemistry, involved in
respiratory and cardiovascular regulation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemoreceptor Trigger Zone} A region near the floor of the fourth ventricle that detects emetic stimuli in blood and
cerebrospinal fluid and helps initiate vomiting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemoresistance} Reduced or absent responsiveness of cells, especially tumor cells, to a chemical
treatment that would ordinarily inhibit or destroy them.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemosensitivity} The degree to which cells or a tumor respond to a chemical treatment. Testing may be
used experimentally or clinically to help characterize treatment response.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemosis} Chemosis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
appearance. It may occur with allergy, infection, inflammation, trauma, or orbital
disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemotaxis} Directed movement of a cell or organism toward or away from a chemical stimulus. It is
important in immune-cell recruitment and inflammatory responses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemotherapy} Chemotherapy is a medical procedure, treatment, device, or care topic; indications, benefits, risks, and follow-up depend on the patient and clinical goal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemotherapy Extravasation} Leakage of an intravenous anticancer drug into surrounding tissue. Vesicant
extravasation can cause severe local injury, so infusion must stop immediately while the
catheter is managed and drug-specific antidote and surgical guidance are obtained.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cherry Angioma} A common benign proliferation of small dermal blood vessels that appears as a bright-red
to purple papule, usually on the trunk or limbs. Lesions may bleed after trauma but
generally require no treatment unless diagnosis is uncertain or removal is desired.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cherry Eye} Prolapse of the gland of the third eyelid, producing a rounded red mass at the inner
corner of the eye. The term is used especially in veterinary ophthalmology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cherry-Red Spot} A striking fundoscopic finding in which the macula appears bright red against a pale,
ischemic, or lipid-laden surrounding retina. Mechanism: the fovea centralis lacks inner
retinal layers and receives blood supply from the underlying choroid, preserving its
natural red color while adjacent edematous retina turns opaque white.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cherubism} A rare inherited disorder characterized by bilateral enlargement of the jaws from
expansile giant-cell lesions, usually beginning in childhood and often regressing after
puberty.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chest Pain} A cardinal symptom with cardiac and non-cardiac causes requiring urgent distinction.
Cardiac: acute coronary syndrome (angina, MI), pericarditis, aortic dissection,
myocarditis. Pulmonary: pulmonary embolism, pneumothorax, pneumonia, pleurisy. GI: GERD,
esophageal spasm, peptic ulcer. Musculoskeletal and psychogenic causes also common.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chest Physiotherapy} Techniques to mobilize and clear secretions from the lungs, including postural drainage,
percussion, vibration, and huffing. It is used in bronchiectasis, CF, and post-operative
patients. Positive expiratory pressure devices and oscillating PEP devices are also
used.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chest Tube Insertion} Placement of a thoracostomy tube in the safe triangle (latissimus dorsi, pectoralis
major, nipple level) to drain air, fluid, or blood from the pleural space. Connected to
underwater seal drainage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chest Tube Thoracostomy} Insertion of a tube into the pleural space to drain air, fluid, or blood. Indications:
pneumothorax (large, tension, or on mechanical ventilation), hemothorax, pleural
effusion (empyema, malignant, transudative), and postoperative (cardiac, thoracic
surgery).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chest Wall} The musculoskeletal boundary of the thoracic cavity, formed by the skin, fascia,
muscles, ribs, costal cartilages, sternum, thoracic vertebrae, and associated
neurovascular structures. It protects the thoracic organs and participates in breathing
through coordinated movement of the ribs and respiratory muscles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chest Wall Deformity} Abnormalities of the chest wall that can affect lung function. Pectus excavatum (sunken
sternum) may compress the heart and lungs. Pectus carinatum (protruding sternum) is
usually cosmetic. Scoliosis can cause restrictive lung disease. These may require
surgical correction if severe.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chest X-ray} Initial imaging for respiratory symptoms. PA and lateral views. Evaluates pneumonia,
pneumothorax, pleural effusion, heart size, mediastinal masses. Limited sensitivity for
early disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chewing} Mastication of food. Teeth, muscles, joints coordinated. Difficulties: dental pain,
missing teeth, TMJ disorder.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cheyne-Stokes Respiration} Crescendo-decrescendo breathing pattern with periods of apnea, seen in heart failure and
stroke. It is a form of central sleep apnea.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chiari I malformation} Downward displacement of the cerebellar tonsils through the foramen magnum, which may cause
headache, neck pain, balance problems, or no symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chiari Malformation} Chiari malformations are structural abnormalities of the posterior fossa and cerebellum
characterized by downward displacement of the cerebellar tonsils through the foramen
magnum. Type I is the most common variant, defined as cerebellar tonsillar ectopia of
five millimeters or more below the foramen magnum.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chiari-Frommel Syndrome} Persistent postpartum secretion of milk with absence of menstruation, associated with
hyperprolactinemia after pregnancy. It is a form of galactorrhea-amenorrhea syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chickenpox} Chickenpox is a contagious varicella-zoster virus infection characterized by an itchy vesicular rash; complications are more likely in adults, pregnancy, immunosuppression, and some infants.
in different stages (macules, papules, vesicles, crusts). Highly contagious via
respiratory droplets and contact with lesions. Incubation 10-21 days. Complications
include secondary bacterial infection, pneumonia, encephalitis, and Reye syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chignon} A temporary swelling (edema) on the baby's scalp caused by the vacuum cap used during
ventouse (vacuum) assisted delivery. The chignon forms as the vacuum creates negative
pressure pulling the scalp tissue into the cup. It settles within 24-48 hours after
birth without any treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chikungunya} An acute mosquito-borne viral infection characterized by fever and often severe joint
pain, with rash and other systemic symptoms in some patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chikungunya Fever} An acute febrile illness caused by chikungunya virus and transmitted primarily by Aedes
mosquitoes. It typically causes abrupt fever, severe symmetrical polyarthralgia,
myalgia, headache, and rash; joint symptoms may persist for months or longer, especially
in older adults or people with pre-existing rheumatic disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chikungunya virus} Chikungunya virus is a mosquito-borne alphavirus that causes abrupt fever and severe joint pain, with arthralgia sometimes persisting for months.
severe joint pain. The virus is transmitted by Aedes mosquitoes. Symptoms include fever,
rash, and polyarthralgia that can be debilitating. Most patients recover fully, but some
develop chronic joint pain lasting months to years.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chilblain} An inflammatory skin lesion caused by an abnormal response to cold and damp conditions.
It produces itchy, red or violaceous swollen areas, usually on fingers, toes, ears, or
nose.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Child Abuse} Physical, sexual, or emotional harm or neglect of a child by a caregiver. Recognized by
inconsistent histories, injury patterns inconsistent with mechanism, and behavioral
indicators. Types: physical abuse, sexual abuse, neglect, and emotional abuse. Mandatory
reporting requirements for healthcare providers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Child Neglect} The failure of a caregiver to provide adequate food, shelter, clothing, supervision,
education, or medical care. The most common form of child maltreatment. Types: physical
neglect, educational neglect, emotional neglect, and medical neglect. May result in
failure to thrive, developmental delay, and chronic illness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Child Poisoning} Exposure of a child to a medicine, chemical, or other substance that may cause harm. Suspected poisoning requires prompt expert
assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Child-Pugh Classification} Clinical scoring system assessing prognosis of chronic liver disease by evaluating five
parameters: total bilirubin, serum albumin, international normalized ratio, ascites, and
hepatic encephalopathy. Each parameter is scored one to three points.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Childbirth} The process of giving birth, encompassing three stages: (1) First stage: from the onset
of regular uterine contractions (cervical effacement and dilation) to full dilation (10
cm). See Parturition (Childbirth).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Childhood Absence Epilepsy} An epilepsy syndrome beginning in childhood with frequent brief absence seizures,
characterized by sudden impairment of awareness and a typical generalized spike-and-wave
EEG pattern.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Childhood Obesity} Excess body fat in a child or adolescent, assessed using age- and sex-specific body-mass-index percentiles.
It reflects interacting genetic, behavioral, environmental, and medical factors and increases later cardiometabolic
risk. Evaluation should be non-stigmatizing and include growth pattern, nutrition, activity, sleep, and comorbidities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chills} Chills is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chimerism} The presence in one individual of cell populations derived from genetically different
zygotes or sources. It may occur naturally, after transplantation, or through exchange
of cells between a pregnant person and fetus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chionablepsia} Temporary visual impairment caused by exposure to intense reflected light from snow or
ice, commonly called snow blindness. It is usually related to ultraviolet injury of the
cornea.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
snow blindness.

\medterm{Chlamydia} Chlamydia is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chlamydia trachomatis infection} A bacterial infection transmitted mainly through sexual contact. It may cause urethritis,
cervicitis, pelvic inflammatory disease, proctitis, or no symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chlamydial pneumonia} Pneumonia caused by Chlamydia species, usually producing a gradual cough, fever, and malaise.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chlamydiosis} Infection caused by bacteria of the genus Chlamydia. Depending on the species and site,
it may cause sexually transmitted infection, conjunctivitis, pneumonia, or other
disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chlamydophila Pneumonia} Atypical pneumonia with mild presentation: sore throat, hoarseness, cough. Treatment is
macrolides or tetracyclines.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chloasma} Patchy brown hyperpigmentation, especially of the face, associated with pregnancy,
hormones, sun exposure, or other causes. It is commonly called melasma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
melasma.

\medterm{Chloracne} An acne-like eruption caused by exposure to certain halogenated aromatic compounds, such
as dioxins and related industrial chemicals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chloramphenicol} A broad-spectrum antibiotic with activity against gram-positive and gram-negative
organisms, as well as Rickettsia. Chloramphenicol inhibits bacterial protein synthesis
by binding to the 50S ribosomal subunit. It is indicated for serious infections when
other agents are not appropriate, including bacterial meningitis and typhoid fever.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chloranemia} Anemia in which the red blood cells are abnormally pale, usually because of reduced
hemoglobin content, as in iron deficiency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chloroleukemia} A rare form of myeloid leukemia associated with greenish tumor masses composed of
myeloid cells, also called granulocytic sarcoma or chloroma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
granulocytic sarcoma or chloroma.

\medterm{Chloroma} A greenish extramedullary mass of immature myeloid cells, usually associated with acute
myeloid leukemia. It is also called granulocytic sarcoma or myeloid sarcoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
granulocytic sarcoma or myeloid sarcoma.

\medterm{Chloropsia} A visual disturbance in which objects appear green or have a greenish tint. It can occur
with retinal disease, medication or toxin exposure, or other visual-pathway disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Choanal Atresia} A congenital obstruction of the posterior nasal passage (choana) due to failure of the
bucconasal membrane to rupture during embryogenesis. Bilateral choanal atresia presents
as a respiratory emergency in the newborn, who is an obligate nasal breather — cyclic
cyanosis that improves with crying.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Choking} Airway obstruction caused by an object lodged in the pharynx or larynx, producing
impaired ventilation. Complete obstruction can cause hypoxic brain injury and death
within minutes; treatment depends on the patient's age, consciousness, and effectiveness
of coughing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholangiitis} Inflammation of the bile ducts. It may be caused by infection, obstruction, autoimmune
disease, or other biliary disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholangiocarcinoma} Cholangiocarcinoma is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
location as intrahepatic, perihilar, or distal extrahepatic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholangiography} Contrast imaging of bile ducts. Intraoperative, percutaneous, or ERCP approach.
Evaluates biliary obstruction, stones, strictures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholangitis} Cholangitis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
(common bile duct stones) obstructing bile flow, but also from biliary strictures, stent
occlusion, and malignancy. The classic clinical presentation is Charcot triad: fever,
right upper quadrant pain, and jaundice (present in 50-70 percent of cases).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholecystectomy} Surgical removal of the gallbladder, usually by a laparoscopic approach when appropriate. It may be performed for symptomatic gallstones, acute cholecystitis, selected gallstone pancreatitis, or other gallbladder disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholecystitis} Inflammation of the gallbladder, most commonly caused by obstruction of the cystic duct
by a gallstone, which occurs in approximately 95 percent of cases. The obstruction leads
to bile stasis, increased intraluminal pressure, and ischemia of the gallbladder wall,
which may progress to bacterial infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholecystography} Radiographic examination of the gallbladder, usually after administration of a contrast
substance, to assess its filling, concentration, and emptying.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholecystokinin} CCK is a peptide hormone produced by I-cells in the duodenal and jejinal mucosa in
response to fatty acids (long-chain fatty acids are the most potent stimuli) and amino
acids in the chyme.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Choledochal Cyst} Congenital dilatation of the biliary tree classified into five types according to the
Todani classification. Type I involving dilation of the common bile duct is the most
common. Presents with the classic triad of abdominal pain, jaundice, and palpable mass
though all three are present in less than twenty percent of cases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Choledocholithiasis} Choledocholithiasis is a disease, symptom, syndrome, or clinical finding; diagnosis and management depend on its characteristic features, causes, severity, examination, and appropriate investigations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholelithiasis} The presence of stones in the gallbladder or biliary tract. Stones may contain cholesterol, pigment, or mixed material and may cause no symptoms or lead to biliary pain and complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholera} Cholera is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholestasis} Cholestasis is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholesteatoma} Cholesteatoma is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Suppurative Otitis Media} Persistent inflammation and infection of the middle ear with recurrent or continuous ear discharge,
usually through a perforated tympanic membrane. It can cause conductive hearing loss and
may lead to cholesteatoma or other complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
CSOM; chronic otitis media with discharge; chronic suppurative middle-ear infection.

\medterm{Cholesterol and Lipids} Fatty substances in the blood, including LDL cholesterol, HDL cholesterol, and
triglycerides, that play critical roles in cellular function and energy metabolism.
Abnormal lipid levels, particularly high LDL and triglycerides with low HDL, promote
atherosclerosis and increase the risk of heart attack and stroke.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholesterol crystal embolization} Atheroembolic disease caused by cholesterol crystal fragments dislodging from proximal
atherosclerotic plaques into distal small arteries, often after vascular procedures.
Presents with livedo reticularis, blue-toe syndrome, renal failure, and eosinophilia.
Diagnosis by skin, kidney, or muscle biopsy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholesterol crystals} Needle-shaped, cleft-like spaces seen in histological sections representing dissolved
cholesterol esters, commonly found in atherosclerotic plaques, atheroembolic disease,
and cholesterol-rich effusions. In atheroembolic disease, cholesterol crystals embolize
from ruptured plaques to distal organs causing tissue ischemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholesterol emboli} Cholesterol crystals released from an atherosclerotic plaque that lodge in small arteries and cause
ischemia or inflammation in downstream tissues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholesterol embolism} Blockage of small arteries by cholesterol crystals, often after vascular instrumentation or plaque
disruption.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholesterol embolization} Release and downstream lodging of cholesterol crystals from an atherosclerotic arterial plaque.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholesterol Synthesis} Occurs in all nucleated cells (primarily liver and intestine, approximately 1 g/day
production), using acetyl-CoA as the precursor through approximately 30 enzymatic steps.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholesterolemia} The presence and concentration of cholesterol in the blood. Abnormal elevation is a risk
factor for atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholinergic Crisis} Excess acetylcholine from organophosphate or carbamate poisoning. SLUDGE syndrome:
salivation, lacrimation, urination, defecation, GI upset, emesis. Miosis, bradycardia,
bronchospasm, muscle fasciculations. Treatment: atropine, pralidoxime.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cholinesterase Inhibitor} A class of medications that inhibit acetylcholinesterase, the enzyme that breaks down
acetylcholine in the synaptic cleft, thereby increasing cholinergic neurotransmission in
the central nervous system and peripheral tissues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chondritis} Inflammation of cartilage, such as the cartilage of the ear, nose, ribs, or larynx. It
may result from infection, trauma, or immune-mediated disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chondroblastic osteosarcoma} A variant of osteosarcoma in which the malignant cells produce a cartilaginous matrix.
It typically affects the long bones of adolescents and young adults, with a predilection
for the metaphyseal regions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chondrocalcinosis} Calcification of cartilage visible on X-ray. Associated with CPPD disease. Found in
knees, wrists, pubic symphysis. May be incidental or symptomatic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chondrodysplasia} A disorder of cartilage and bone development that causes abnormal skeletal growth and,
in some forms, disproportionate short stature.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chondroma} Benign cartilage-forming tumor composed of mature hyaline cartilage, most often
intramedullary (enchondroma) or subperiosteal (juxtacortical / periosteal chondroma).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chondromalacia} Softening and degeneration of cartilage, especially articular cartilage. The term is
often used for changes affecting the patella and causing anterior knee pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chondrosarcoma} The second most common primary malignant bone tumor (after osteosarcoma) and the most
common primary malignant bone tumor in adults (peak age 40-70 years).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chopart's Joint} The transverse tarsal joint, consisting of the talonavicular and calcaneocuboid joints,
between the hindfoot and the midfoot.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chordae Tendineae} Thin tendinous cords connecting the papillary muscles to the free edges of the
atrioventricular valve leaflets (mitral and tricuspid). They tether the leaflets and,
with papillary muscle contraction during ventricular systole, prevent valve prolapse and
regurgitation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chordoma} Slow-growing, locally aggressive malignant bone tumor originating from embryonic
remnants of the notochord along the axial skeleton. Three anatomic locations: (1)
sacrococcygeal ( 50\% of cases, most common), (2) spheno-occipital/clival ( 35\%, base
of skull), (3) vertebral (cervical thoracolumbar, 15\%).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chorea} Involuntary, irregular, brief, non-repetitive jerky movements flowing randomly from one
body part to another ('dance-like'), due to basal ganglia (striatal) dysfunction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chorioamnionitis} Infection of the chorion and amnion, typically due to ascending bacterial infection from
the genital tract. Risk factors include prolonged rupture of membranes, multiple vaginal
exams, and preterm labor. Presents with maternal fever, uterine tenderness, and fetal
tachycardia. Diagnosis is clinical; amniocentesis for confirmation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chorioamnionitis (Intraamniotic Infection)} Infection or inflammation of the amniotic fluid, membranes, placenta, or decidua,
usually due to ascending polymicrobial infection. Maternal fever, uterine tenderness,
fetal tachycardia, and maternal leukocytosis may occur; treatment requires
broad-spectrum antibiotics and delivery planning.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Intraamniotic Infection

\medterm{Choriocarcinoma} Choriocarcinoma is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
cytotrophoblast, the most aggressive form of gestational trophoblastic disease (GTD).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chorion} The outermost fetal membrane, derived from the trophoblast. The chorion frondosum
(villous chorion) develops into the fetal portion of the placenta. The chorion laeve
(smooth chorion) is the non-villous portion. Chorionic villi contain fetal blood vessels
and are bathed in maternal blood.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chorionic Villus Sampling} Prenatal diagnostic test at 10-13 weeks gestation, earlier than amniocentesis.
Transabdominal or transcervical biopsy of chorionic villi (placental tissue).
Indications: advanced maternal age, abnormal first-trimester screen, carrier screening,
or family history of genetic disorders. Analyzed for karyotype, FISH, and microarray.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chorioretinitis} Inflammation involving the choroid and retina, often caused by infection, autoimmune
disease, or systemic inflammatory illness. It can produce floaters and impaired vision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Choroid} Choroid is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Choroid Plexus Cyst} A fluid-filled cyst within the choroid plexus of a fetal cerebral ventricle. An isolated cyst
with otherwise reassuring prenatal assessment is usually benign, but its significance
depends on other ultrasound findings and screening results.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Choroid Plexus Tumor} A papillary neoplasm of the choroid plexus, occurring most often in children, and
frequently presenting with hydrocephalus from excessive cerebrospinal fluid production.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Choroidal Neovascularization} The pathological growth of new, fragile blood vessels from the choroid through Bruch
membrane into the subretinal or sub-retinal pigment epithelium space, and it is the
hallmark of wet age-related macular degeneration and the primary cause of severe central
vision loss in this condition.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Christmas Disease} Hemophilia B, an inherited bleeding disorder caused by deficiency or dysfunction of
coagulation factor IX.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chromaffinoma} A tumor of chromaffin cells, usually a pheochromocytoma of the adrenal medulla or a
related extra-adrenal paraganglioma. It may secrete catecholamines and cause episodic
hypertension.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chromatin} The complex of DNA, histone proteins, and associated molecules that packages the genome
in the nucleus. Its organization and chemical modification regulate DNA accessibility,
replication, repair, and transcription.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chromatopsia} A visual disturbance in which objects appear abnormally colored. It may result from
retinal disease, toxic exposure, medication effects, or other disorders of the visual
system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chromhidrosis} A rare condition in which sweat is abnormally colored, often yellow, green, blue, or
black, because of pigment in the sweat glands or on the skin surface.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chromoblastomycosis} A chronic fungal infection of skin and subcutaneous tissue that produces slowly enlarging warty or
scaly lesions, usually after traumatic implantation of the fungus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chromosomal Abnormality} Change in chromosome number or structure. Numerical: aneuploidy (abnormal number, e.g.,
trisomy 21), polyploidy (extra sets). Structural: deletion, duplication, inversion,
translocation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chromosome} Chromosome concerns chromosome, a body structure, substance, or physiologic concept whose clinical meaning depends on location, function, and patient context.
information. Humans have 46 chromosomes (23 pairs): 22 pairs of autosomes, 1 pair of sex
chromosomes (XX female, XY male). Chromosomes condense during cell division for
visualization.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic} Chronic is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Allograft Nephropathy} Progressive transplant kidney function decline over months to years. Interstitial
fibrosis and tubular atrophy on histology. Can be immunologic or non-immunologic.
Optimize immunosuppression and manage risk factors including hypertension and recurrent
disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic autoimmune Hashimoto thyroiditis} An autoimmune inflammatory disease of the thyroid that progressively destroys thyroid
tissue and commonly causes hypothyroidism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic autoimmune thyroiditis} Hashimoto thyroiditis, an autoimmune destruction of the thyroid that commonly causes
hypothyroidism and may be associated with anti-thyroid antibodies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Bronchitis} Defined clinically as a productive cough for at least three months in each of two
consecutive years, after excluding other causes. It is characterized by hypertrophy of
mucus-secreting glands in the bronchi (Reid index > 0.5), goblet cell hyperplasia, and
chronic airway inflammation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Caries} Tooth decay that progresses slowly and may produce a firm, darkened lesion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Cough} Cough lasting > 8 weeks. Top 3 causes in non-smokers with normal CXR: upper airway cough
syndrome, asthma, GERD. ACE inhibitor use is another common cause.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Eosinophilic Leukaemia} A clonal myeloid neoplasm characterized by persistent eosinophilia, increased or
dysplastic eosinophils, and evidence of an underlying clonal process or organ injury.
Symptoms may include fatigue, fever, weight loss, rash, respiratory symptoms,
splenomegaly, or cardiac and neurological complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic eosinophilic pneumonia} An idiopathic inflammatory lung disease characterized by eosinophilic infiltration of
the pulmonary parenchyma, presenting with fever, cough, dyspnea, and peripheral airspace
opacities on imaging.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Fatigue Syndrome} A complex, debilitating disorder characterized by profound fatigue lasting >6 months
that is not relieved by rest and is worsened by physical or mental exertion
(post-exertional malaise). Associated symptoms: unrefreshing sleep, cognitive impairment
(brain fog), orthostatic intolerance, joint pain, headaches, and sore throat.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Granulomatous Disease} A primary immunodeficiency caused by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex in phagocytes
(neutrophils, monocytes, macrophages), impairing the respiratory burst and killing of
catalase-positive organisms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Hypertension in Pregnancy} Hypertension that predates pregnancy or is diagnosed early in pregnancy.
It increases the risk of preeclampsia, fetal growth problems, preterm birth, and placental complications and requires
ongoing maternal and fetal monitoring.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Inflammation} A prolonged inflammatory response characterized by simultaneous tissue destruction and
attempts at repair, lasting weeks to years. Unlike acute inflammation, it is
predominantly mononuclear, involving macrophages, lymphocytes, and plasma cells rather
than neutrophils.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Inflammatory Demyelinating Polyneuropathy} An acquired, immune-mediated peripheral neuropathy with a relapsing-remitting or
progressive course. Presents with symmetric proximal and distal weakness, sensory loss,
areflexia, and elevated CSF protein. Distinguishing from Guillain-Barre: progressive
course >8 weeks (vs. rapid onset in GBS).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Inflammatory Response Syndrome} A proposed multisystem illness involving persistent inflammatory symptoms after an exposure
such as certain water-damaged building environments. Reported symptoms are nonspecific and may include fatigue,
headache, cognitive difficulty, and respiratory complaints. Symptoms require a careful medical evaluation for
established infectious, allergic, toxic, autoimmune, and other causes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Intestinal Pseudo-Obstruction} Chronic condition of recurrent episodes of intestinal obstruction without mechanical
blockage due to neuromuscular dysfunction of the gastrointestinal tract. May involve the
small bowel, colon, or both. Causes include systemic sclerosis, diabetes, Parkinson
disease, amyloidosis, and paraneoplastic syndromes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Kidney Disease} Chronic kidney disease is the progressive loss of kidney function over months to years,
typically from diabetes, hypertension, or glomerular diseases. It is staged by estimated
glomerular filtration rate and albuminuria, with end-stage renal disease requiring
dialysis or transplantation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Kidney Disease Staging} Classification of kidney disease by cause, eGFR category, and albuminuria category. G1
through G5 represent eGFR from normal or high to kidney failure; staging is applied when
abnormalities persist for at least 3 months and helps estimate progression and
complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic lead poisoning} Prolonged low-level lead exposure causing abdominal pain, anemia with basophilic
stippling, peripheral neuropathy, and nephropathy, requiring chelation therapy and
removal of the exposure source.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Leukemia} A leukemia characterized by an insidious onset, clonal proliferation of relatively mature
blood cells, and a more indolent clinical course.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Limb-Threatening Ischemia} The most severe clinical form of peripheral arterial disease, characterized by chronic
ischemic rest pain, nonhealing ulceration, or gangrene attributable to arterial
insufficiency. It carries high risks of limb loss and cardiovascular mortality and
requires vascular evaluation for revascularization and wound management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Liver Disease} Persistent hepatic injury and structural change lasting at least 6 months, caused by
viral, metabolic, alcohol-related, autoimmune, cholestatic, vascular, or drug-related
disease. Progressive fibrosis can lead to cirrhosis, portal hypertension, liver failure,
and hepatocellular carcinoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Lymphocytic Leukemia} The most common leukemia in Western countries, characterized by the clonal proliferation
of mature-appearing but functionally incompetent B lymphocytes that accumulate in blood,
bone marrow, and lymphoid tissues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Mesenteric Ischemia} Recurrent abdominal pain occurring after eating resulting from insufficient blood flow
to meet the metabolic demands of the gastrointestinal tract. Most commonly caused by
atherosclerotic stenosis of at least two of the three major mesenteric arteries.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Migraine} A migraine disorder with headache on at least 15 days per month for more than three months, with migraine features
on at least eight days per month. Symptoms may include throbbing pain, nausea, vomiting, and light or sound
sensitivity; frequent or disabling headaches require clinical evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Mucocutaneous Candidiasis} Persistent/recurrent Candida infections of skin, nails, mucosa (oral, esophageal,
vaginal) without systemic dissemination. Autosomal dominant (STAT1 gain-of-function) or
recessive (IL17F, IL17RA, ACT1, RORC). Associated with autoimmunity (hypothyroidism,
AIHA, ITP, alopecia), endocrinopathies, Staph infections.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic myelogenous leukemia} A myeloproliferative neoplasm associated with the BCR-ABL1 fusion gene, usually
producing marked leukocytosis and splenomegaly.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Myeloid Leukemia} A myeloproliferative neoplasm characterized by the Philadelphia chromosome
[t(9;22)(q34;q11.2)], which creates the BCR-ABL1 fusion gene encoding a constitutively
active tyrosine kinase.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Myelomonocytic Leukemia} A rare myelodysplastic/myeloproliferative neoplasm characterized by persistent
monocytosis (absolute monocyte count greater than 1,000 per microliter and greater than
10 percent of white blood cells) in the peripheral blood and bone marrow, with
dysplastic features and variable blast count.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Neutrophilic Leukaemia} A rare clonal myeloproliferative neoplasm characterized by sustained mature neutrophilic
leukocytosis without a primary infectious or inflammatory cause. It may cause fatigue,
fever, weight loss, splenomegaly, and progressive constitutional symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease} COPD is a preventable lung disease characterized by persistent respiratory symptoms and
airflow limitation due to airway and/or alveolar abnormalities, usually caused by
significant exposure to noxious particles or gases (primarily tobacco smoke). Includes
emphysema and chronic bronchitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Pain} Pain persisting longer than three months or beyond normal tissue healing time, often
lacking an ongoing identifiable cause. It involves complex interactions between
biological, psychological, and social factors that can amplify pain signaling in the
nervous system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Pain Management} Chronic pain management extends beyond the perioperative period. Opioid therapy for
chronic pain requires careful patient selection, informed consent, and regular
reassessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Pancreatitis} Progressive irreversible inflammatory condition characterized by permanent structural
damage causing exocrine and endocrine impairment. The most common cause is chronic
alcohol consumption. Other causes include hereditary pancreatitis, autoimmune
pancreatitis, and metabolic factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Rhinitis} Persistent inflammation or irritation of the nasal lining causing congestion, runny nose, sneezing, itching, or
postnasal drainage. Allergic, nonallergic, medication-related, and structural causes are possible. Management depends
on the cause and may include trigger avoidance, saline irrigation, and clinician-directed nasal treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Rhinosinusitis} Persistent inflammation of the nose and paranasal sinuses lasting at least 12 weeks, usually
with nasal obstruction or discharge and sometimes facial pressure or reduced smell. It
may occur with or without nasal polyps.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic sinusitis} Persistent inflammation of the paranasal sinuses for at least 12 weeks, causing nasal
obstruction, discharge, facial pressure, and reduced smell.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Tension-Type Headache} A recurring headache occurring on at least 15 days per month for more than three months, usually causing
bilateral pressing or tightening pain rather than throbbing. It may be associated with scalp or neck tenderness
but typically lacks prominent nausea; a new or changing pattern requires clinical assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Thromboembolic Pulmonary Hypertension} Pulmonary hypertension caused by organized, persistent thromboembolic obstruction and
secondary pulmonary-vessel remodeling after pulmonary embolism. It is potentially
curable in selected patients by pulmonary endarterectomy or balloon pulmonary
angioplasty.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Toxicity} Adverse effects from repeated or prolonged exposure to low doses. Cumulative damage may
be irreversible. Examples: heavy metals (lead, mercury), asbestos, alcohol, solvent
exposure. Monitoring: biomarkers, organ function tests.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Venous Disease} A spectrum of venous outflow disorders caused by reflux, obstruction, or
calf-muscle-pump dysfunction. Findings range from edema and varicose veins to skin
changes, lipodermatosclerosis, and venous ulceration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Venous Insufficiency} Impaired venous return from the lower extremities due to venous valve incompetence or
obstruction. Leads to venous hypertension, edema, skin changes (lipodermatosclerosis,
hyperpigmentation), and venous stasis ulcers (medial malleolus). Risk factors: varicose
veins, DVT, obesity, prolonged standing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic venous stasis} Persistent pooling of blood in the lower limbs caused by impaired venous return. It can produce
edema, skin discoloration, dermatitis, and venous ulcers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Churg-Strauss Syndrome} Eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis, a necrotizing inflammation of small and
medium blood vessels associated with asthma, eosinophilia, and possible injury to the
lungs, nerves, skin, heart, or kidneys.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chvostek Sign} A clinical sign of latent tetany caused by hypocalcemia (ionized $\mathrm{Ca^{2+}} < 4.4\,\mathrm{mg/dL}$).
Elicited by tapping the facial nerve over the parotid gland just anterior to the ear
canal. Positive response: involuntary twitching or contraction of ipsilateral facial
muscles (lip twitching or facial spasm).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chylangioma} A benign malformation or tumor-like lesion of lymphatic vessels containing chyle or
lymph. It may occur in the neck, mediastinum, or other tissues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chylomicron} Transports dietary lipids. Largest, least dense lipoprotein.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chylothorax} Pleural effusion with chyle from thoracic duct disruption. Triglycerides > 110 mg/dL.
Treatment includes dietary modification, octreotide, and surgical ligation if
refractory.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chyluria} Passage of lymphatic fluid into the urine, giving it a milky appearance. It usually
results from an abnormal communication between lymphatic vessels and the urinary tract.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Ciclopia} A rare severe congenital malformation in which the eyes are fused or a single median eye
develops, usually as part of holoprosencephaly.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CIDP} Chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy (CIDP) is a chronic, immune-mediated,
peripheral neuropathy characterized by progressive or relapsing symmetrical weakness and
sensory loss in all four limbs, with areflexia or hyporeflexia, lasting for more than
eight weeks.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Ciguatera} Food poisoning caused by eating reef fish containing ciguatoxins. It may cause
gastrointestinal illness followed by neurologic symptoms such as paresthesias and
temperature-sensation disturbance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Ciliary Body} Circumferential structure between iris and choroid, with ciliary muscle (accommodation
control via zonular fibers) and ciliary processes (aqueous humor production). Part of
the uveal tract (iris, ciliary body, choroid).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Ciliopathy} A disorder caused by abnormal structure or function of cellular cilia. Ciliopathies may
affect development, vision, kidney function, respiration, or multiple organ systems.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cineangiocardiography} Radiographic recording of the heart and blood vessels during passage of contrast
material, used to visualize cardiac chambers, valves, and coronary or great vessels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cinefluorography} Motion-picture recording of fluoroscopic images, used to document movement of organs or
contrast material during a diagnostic examination.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cineplasty} Surgical creation of a muscle-controlled stump or related reconstruction, historically
used to improve control of a prosthetic limb.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cingulotomy} Surgical interruption of selected fibers of the cingulum, sometimes performed as a
psychosurgical treatment for severe, treatment-resistant psychiatric illness or chronic
pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Circadian Rhythm} The approximately 24-hour endogenous biological cycle regulating sleep-wake behavior,
hormone secretion, body temperature, metabolism, and other physiological processes.
Generated by the suprachiasmatic nucleus of the hypothalamus, which is entrained to the
environment primarily by light via the retinohypothalamic tract.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Circle of Willis} A ring-like arterial anastomosis at the base of the brain that provides collateral blood
flow between the anterior (internal carotid) and posterior (vertebrobasilar)
circulations. It is formed by the anterior cerebral, anterior communicating, internal
carotid, posterior cerebral, and posterior communicating arteries.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Circulating Tumor DNA} Fragments of tumor-derived DNA detectable in blood, enabling molecular profiling,
treatment monitoring, and detection of minimal residual disease without tissue biopsy
(liquid biopsy).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Circumcision} Circumcision is a procedure for circumcision; indication, preparation, risks, technical details, and recovery depend on the patient and treatment goal.
medical reasons such as severe phimosis, recurrent balanoposthitis, paraphimosis, or
lichen sclerosus (balanitis xerotica obliterans); elective infant circumcision for
religious, cultural, or preventive health reasons.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Circumflex Artery} A branch of the left coronary artery that travels in the left atrioventricular groove.
It supplies the lateral wall of the left ventricle, the left atrium, and in
left-dominant systems, the inferior wall and posteromedial papillary muscle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cirrhosis} Cirrhosis is advanced liver scarring with regenerative nodules that distorts blood flow and liver function, potentially causing portal hypertension, jaundice, ascites, and liver failure.
distortion of the hepatic architecture and formation of regenerative nodules. The most
common causes include chronic hepatitis C infection, alcoholic liver disease,
non-alcoholic steatohepatitis, chronic hepatitis B infection, and autoimmune hepatitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Citrate Synthase} First enzyme of TCA cycle. Condenses oxaloacetate and acetyl-CoA to citrate.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Citric Acid Cycle (Krebs Cycle)} A central metabolic pathway occurring in the mitochondrial matrix that oxidizes
acetyl-CoA (derived from carbohydrates, lipids, and proteins) into CO2, generating 3
NADH, 1 FADH2, and 1 GTP (ATP) per turn. Rate-limiting enzyme: isocitrate dehydrogenase
(inhibited by ATP and NADH; activated by ADP).



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Krebs Cycle
