for hemodynamically unstable patients with severe AKI.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Continuous Murmurs} Murmurs that persist throughout systole and diastole without interruption, produced by
blood flowing continuously through an abnormal connection. The most common cause is
patent ductus arteriosus (PDA), where blood flows from the aorta to pulmonary artery
throughout the cardiac cycle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Continuous Positive Airway Pressure} Noninvasive respiratory support that delivers a constant positive pressure throughout
the breathing cycle to keep the upper airway or alveoli open.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contraception} Methods to prevent pregnancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contraception and Birth Control} Contraception encompasses methods used to prevent pregnancy, including hormonal options
like oral contraceptives, patches, rings, injections, and implants, as well as
non-hormonal methods like copper IUDs, barrier methods, diaphragms, and permanent
sterilization.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contraception for Adolescents} Methods to prevent unintended pregnancy. Options: combined oral contraceptives,
progestin-only pills, depot medroxyprogesterone acetate, contraceptive implant
(Nexplanon), intrauterine devices (IUDs), condoms, and abstinence.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contracted Pelvis} A pelvis whose size or shape is reduced or abnormal enough to interfere with passage of
the fetus during labor.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contraction Stress Test} An antepartum test that evaluates the fetal heart rate response to uterine contractions.
Contractions are induced with oxytocin or nipple stimulation (until 3 contractions in 10
minutes). Negative (normal): no late or significant variable decelerations. Positive
(abnormal): late decelerations with \(\geq 50\%\) of contractions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contractions} Rhythmic uterine muscular contractions of labor that produce cervical dilation and
effacement, driving descent of the fetus. Characterized by frequency, duration, and
intensity; progress is monitored by partogram and tocography (CTG). Braxton Hicks
contractions: irregular, non-progressive, painless prelabor contractions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contracture} Permanent or semi-permanent limitation of passive joint range of motion caused by
shortening or structural change in muscle, tendon, capsule, fascia, skin, or other
periarticular tissue. Prevention and treatment depend on cause and stage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contracture Prevention} Measures such as positioning, stretching, exercise, splinting, orthoses, and serial casting that help preserve joint movement in people at risk of fixed shortening.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contraindication} Contraindication is a biological, genetic, biochemical, or chemical term used in medicine; its significance depends on its structure, function, concentration, pathway, or clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contralateral} Located on the opposite side of the body.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contrast Agent} Substance enhancing image visibility. X-ray/CT: iodine-based (positive contrast). MRI:
gadolinium-based (shortens T1, bright on T1). Ultrasound: microbubble agents. Barium
sulfate for GI studies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contrast Echocardiography} Use of microbubble contrast agents to enhance endocardial border definition and assess
myocardial perfusion. Indicated for suboptimal non-contrast echo images and assessment
of myocardial viability. Microbubbles remain entirely within the intravascular space,
making them ideal for perfusion assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contrast Reaction} Adverse reaction to contrast agent. Mild: nausea, urticaria. Severe: anaphylaxis,
bronchospasm. Pre-medication for prior reactions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contrast-Associated Acute Kidney Injury} Acute worsening of kidney function occurring after administration of contrast, whether
or not contrast is causal. Risk assessment includes baseline kidney function,
hemodynamic status, diabetes, nephrotoxins, urgency, and the need to avoid unnecessary
contrast exposure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contrast-Enhanced Ultrasound} Ultrasound using microbubble contrast agents. Improves lesion characterization,
perfusion assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contrast-Induced Nephropathy} Acute kidney injury after iodinated contrast administration. Risk factors: pre-existing
renal impairment, diabetes, dehydration. Prevention: hydration, lowest contrast volume,
N-acetylcysteine controversial.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contrecoup} An injury occurring on the side of an organ opposite the site of impact, especially a
brain injury produced when the brain moves within the skull after trauma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Control Group} The comparison group in a study, which may receive usual care, a placebo, or no intervention according to the study design.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Controlled Cord Traction} A technique for active management of third-stage labor: gentle, steady traction on the
umbilical cord with one hand while the other hand applies suprapubic counter-pressure
(to prevent uterine inversion). Performed after signs of placental separation. The
Brandt-Andrews maneuver and the Finocchietti technique are CCT methods.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Controlled Substance} A drug or chemical whose manufacture, possession, and use are regulated by government
authorities due to its potential for abuse, dependence, or harm.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Contusion} A closed soft-tissue injury caused by blunt force, producing bleeding into tissues
without an open wound. It commonly causes pain, swelling, and ecchymosis; severe
contusions may involve muscle necrosis, compartment syndrome, internal bleeding, or
associated bone and organ injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Conus Medullaris Syndrome} A clinical condition resulting from damage to the conus medullaris, the tapered,
terminal end of the spinal cord located at approximately the L1-L2 vertebral level.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Convergence Insufficiency} Inability to maintain binocular convergence on near objects, causing eyestrain,
headaches, blurred near vision, and transient diplopia during reading. Common in
children and young adults; treated with convergence exercises and base-in prism, with
surgery rarely needed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coombs Test} A laboratory test that detects antibodies or complement attached to red blood cells, or
antibodies in serum that can react with red cells. It is used in evaluating immune
hemolysis and blood compatibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cooper's Ligament} Fibrous connective-tissue septa extending from the deep surface of the breast toward the
skin and pectoral fascia. Tumor invasion or fibrosis may cause skin retraction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coordination} Smooth, efficient movement. Tests: finger-to-nose, heel-to-shin, rapid alternating
movements.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{COPD} Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease is a progressive, largely irreversible airflow
limitation caused by chronic bronchitis, emphysema, or both, most commonly due to
cigarette smoking. It presents with chronic cough, sputum production, and exertional
dyspnea. Spirometry shows a reduced FEV1/FVC ratio.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{COPD Exacerbation} Acute worsening of respiratory symptoms beyond normal day-to-day variation in a patient
with COPD that leads to additional therapy. Common triggers include viral or bacterial
infection, air pollution, and cardiopulmonary comorbidity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{COPD Rehabilitation} Comprehensive intervention for COPD patients including exercise training, education,
behavior change. Components: aerobic exercise, strength training, breathing techniques,
nutrition, psychosocial support. Improves exercise capacity, quality of life, reduces
hospitalizations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Copper} An essential trace element required for iron transport, connective-tissue cross-linking,
energy metabolism, neurotransmitter synthesis, and antioxidant enzymes. Deficiency can
cause anemia, neutropenia, impaired neurologic function, and bone abnormalities; excess
is toxic and accumulates in Wilson disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{copy number variation} Deletions or duplications of DNA segments >1 kb. Contribute to genetic diversity and
disease. Examples: 22q11.2 deletion, Williams syndrome (7q11.23 deletion).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cor Pulmonale} Right ventricular hypertrophy and dilation from pulmonary hypertension due to lung
disease. Features include peripheral edema, JVD, and hepatomegaly.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cor Triatriatum} A rare congenital heart defect where the left atrium is divided into two chambers by a
fibromuscular membrane, separating the pulmonary venous chamber (posterior) from the
true left atrium (anterior) containing the left atrial appendage and mitral valve. The
membrane obstructs pulmonary venous return, mimicking mitral stenosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coracoid Process} Hook-like scapular projection extending anteriorly and laterally. Attachment for 3
muscles (pectoralis minor, coracobrachialis, short head of biceps) and 3 ligaments.
Important surgical landmark. Fractures uncommon but may occur with direct trauma or
shoulder dislocations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cord Presentation} The presence of the umbilical cord between the presenting fetal part and the cervix,
with intact membranes. Risk factors: malpresentation, unstable lie, low-lying placenta,
and long cord. If the membranes rupture, the cord may prolapse (umbilical cord
prolapse).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cordocentesis} A prenatal diagnostic procedure in which fetal blood is sampled from the umbilical cord,
usually under ultrasound guidance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cordotomy} Surgical division of selected nerve fibers, especially a spinal cord tract, to interrupt
transmission of severe chronic pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Core Biopsy} Removal of a cylindrical sample of tissue with a hollow cutting needle for microscopic
examination, commonly used for breast, liver, prostate, and other lesions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Core Needle Biopsy} Biopsy using core needle. CT, ultrasound, or MRI guidance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cori Cycle} Lactate produced by muscles transported to liver, converted to glucose via
gluconeogenesis, returned to muscles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cornea} Transparent, avascular, dome-shaped anterior eye surface providing approximately
two-thirds of total refractive power. Five layers: epithelium (stratified squamous,
rapidly regenerating), Bowman's membrane, stroma (thickest, regular collagen),
Descemet's membrane, endothelium (single cell layer maintaining transparency).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corneal Abrasion} Loss of corneal epithelium caused by mechanical trauma — contact with fingernail, makeup
brush, foreign body, contact lens overwear, dry eye, sports injury, paper edge, or
surgical instrumentation. Most common eye injury presenting to emergency departments.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corneal Cross-Linking} Corneal collagen cross-linking is a minimally invasive therapeutic procedure that
strengthens the corneal stroma by creating additional chemical bonds between collagen
fibrils, thereby stiffening the cornea and halting or slowing the progression of corneal
ectatic disorders, particularly keratoconus and post-refractive surgery ectasia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corneal Edema} Excess fluid within the corneal stroma or epithelium causing corneal thickening, reduced
transparency, glare, and blurred vision. Causes include endothelial dysfunction, acute
glaucoma, inflammation, trauma, and postoperative injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corneal Implant} An implanted device or tissue substitute placed in or on the cornea to restore vision,
replace diseased tissue, or alter corneal shape.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corneal Ulcer} A corneal ulcer is a defect in the corneal epithelium with associated stromal
inflammation and necrosis, typically caused by infectious or non-infectious mechanisms,
and it is a sight-threatening condition that requires prompt diagnosis and treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corneitis} Inflammation of the cornea, often producing pain, photophobia, tearing, and impaired
vision. It may be infectious, immune-mediated, traumatic, or related to exposure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cornelia de Lange Syndrome} A genetic disorder caused by mutations in genes of the cohesin complex (NIPBL 60\%,
SMC1A, SMC3, RAD21, HDAC8).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corns and Calluses} Corns (heloma) are focal areas of hyperkeratosis caused by pressure and friction,
typically on toes. Hard corns (heloma durum) occur on dorsal toes; soft corns (heloma
molle) occur between toes. Calluses (tyloma) are diffuse hyperkeratosis on
weight-bearing surfaces.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronal Caries} Caries on clinical crown. Smooth surface or pits/fissures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronal Plane} A vertical plane that divides the body or an organ into front (anterior) and back
(posterior) portions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Angiography} Invasive imaging of the coronary arteries using catheter-delivered contrast and X-ray
fluoroscopy. It shows coronary anatomy and stenosis and can support treatment planning;
noninvasive tests may be appropriate in other clinical settings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Arteries} The right and left arteries arising from the aortic sinuses that supply the myocardium.
Their branches include the right coronary, anterior interventricular, and circumflex
arteries.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Artery} One of the blood vessels branching from the aorta that supplies oxygen-rich blood
directly to the heart muscle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Artery Aneurysm} A localized dilatation of a coronary artery exceeding the normal vessel diameter by
approximately 1.5 times. It may be caused by atherosclerosis, Kawasaki disease,
connective-tissue disorders, infection, trauma, or an iatrogenic procedure, and can lead
to thrombosis, distal embolization, myocardial ischemia, or rupture.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Artery Bypass} A surgical procedure that creates a new route for blood flow around a narrowed or
blocked coronary artery using a vascular graft.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Artery Bypass Grafting} A surgical revascularization procedure for severe coronary artery disease (CAD). Grafts,
most commonly the left internal mammary artery (LIMA) and saphenous vein grafts (SVG),
are used to bypass stenotic coronary arterial segments. Indicated for multi-vessel CAD,
left main coronary artery stenosis, or severe CAD in diabetic patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Artery Disease} Narrowing or occlusion of coronary arteries, usually due to atherosclerosis, reducing
blood flow to the myocardium. Risk factors include hypertension, diabetes,
hyperlipidemia, smoking, family history, and obesity. CAD can present as stable angina,
acute coronary syndromes (unstable angina, NSTEMI, STEMI), or sudden cardiac death.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Artery Spasm} Transient intense constriction of an epicardial coronary artery that reduces myocardial
blood flow. It may cause rest angina and transient ST-segment elevation and can be
triggered by smoking, stimulants, or endothelial dysfunction; calcium-channel blockers
and nitrates are commonly used for prevention and relief.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Calcium Score} A CT-based measurement of coronary artery calcification, quantified as the Agatston
score. A score of 0 indicates very low risk of cardiovascular events; >400 indicates
high burden and significant atherosclerosis. It is used for cardiovascular risk
stratification in asymptomatic individuals and may guide statin therapy decisions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Calcium Scoring} Non-contrast CT measuring calcified plaque in coronary arteries. Agatston score predicts
cardiovascular risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Circulation} The vascular system supplying blood to the myocardium. The left coronary artery (LCA)
divides into the left anterior descending (LAD) and circumflex (LCx) arteries. The right
coronary artery (RCA) supplies the right ventricle, SA node (in 60\% of people), and
inferior left ventricular wall.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Dominance} Determined by which artery gives rise to the posterior descending artery (PDA). Right
coronary dominance (85\% of people): RCA gives rise to PDA. Left coronary dominance
(8\%): LCx gives rise to PDA. Codominance (7\%): both RCA and LCx contribute.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Heart Disease} Disease caused by reduced blood flow through the coronary arteries, usually due to
atherosclerotic plaque. It may cause angina, myocardial infarction, heart failure, or
sudden cardiac death. Also called coronary artery disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
coronary artery disease.

\medterm{Coronary Insufficiency} Inadequate blood flow through the coronary circulation to meet myocardial oxygen demand,
usually because of coronary artery disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Microvascular Dysfunction} Impairment of the small coronary arteries and arterioles causing impaired coronary flow
reserve despite normal epicardial coronary arteries. Common in women, patients with
diabetes, and hypertension. Causes chest pain (angina equivalent) and may contribute to
heart failure with preserved ejection fraction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Occlusion} Obstruction of a coronary artery, commonly by atherosclerotic plaque and thrombus, that
can cause myocardial ischemia or infarction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Sinus} A large vein on the back of the heart that collects deoxygenated blood from the heart
muscle and drains it into the right atrium.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Stent} Mesh tube keeping coronary artery open after angioplasty. Types: bare-metal (higher
restenosis), drug-eluting (releases antiproliferative drug, lower restenosis but
prolonged DAPT). Complications: stent thrombosis, restenosis, bleeding (from DAPT).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Thrombosis} Formation of a blood clot in a coronary artery, potentially obstructing blood flow and causing
myocardial infarction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronavirus Disease 2019} A respiratory and systemic infectious disease caused by SARS-CoV-2. Illness ranges from
asymptomatic infection to pneumonia, respiratory failure, and multisystem complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corpse} The dead body of a human being; the subject of medicolegal examination. Forensic
assessment of a corpse includes identification, determination of death and time of death
(rigor mortis, livor mortis, algor mortis, vitreous potassium, gastric emptying),
external and internal examination, and collection of evidence.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corpus Callosum} Brain's largest white matter tract with approximately 200 million axons connecting left
and right hemispheres. Four parts: rostrum, genu, body, splenium. Facilitates
interhemispheric communication for sensory, motor, and cognitive integration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corrected Age} The age of a preterm infant calculated by subtracting the number of weeks born before 40
weeks of gestation from chronological age. Corrected age helps interpret growth and
developmental milestones during early childhood, commonly until about 2 years of age.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corrigan Pulse} A forceful, rapidly rising and collapsing arterial pulse, classically associated with
aortic regurgitation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corrosive} Substance causing tissue destruction by chemical action. Acids: coagulation necrosis,
eschar. Alkalis: liquefaction necrosis, deep penetration. Ingestion: avoid emesis,
endoscopy, surgical consultation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corti's Ganglion} The spiral ganglion of the cochlea, containing the cell bodies of sensory neurons that
transmit auditory information from the organ of Corti.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cortical Cataract} A type of age-related lens opacity that begins in the peripheral lens cortex as
wedge-shaped or spoke-like opacities that extend centripetally toward the visual axis,
caused by disruption of the normally orderly arrangement of lens fibers and the
accumulation of fluid between them.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corticobasal Degeneration} A rare, progressive neurodegenerative disorder characterized by asymmetric cortical and
basal ganglia dysfunction. Presents with asymmetric parkinsonism (rigidity,
bradykinesia), apraxia (loss of learned motor skills), alien limb phenomenon
(involuntary, purposeless movements), cortical sensory loss, myoclonus, and dystonia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corticosteroids} Synthetic glucocorticoids with potent anti-inflammatory and immunosuppressive effects.
Examples include prednisone, prednisolone, methylprednisolone, dexamethasone, and
hydrocortisone. They suppress inflammatory gene expression and inhibit immune cell
function.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Corticosteroids Mechanism} Corticosteroids exert their anti-inflammatory and immunosuppressive effects primarily by
binding to intracellular glucocorticoid receptors, which then translocate to the nucleus
and modulate gene transcription.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cortisol} The primary glucocorticoid produced by the adrenal cortex (zona fasciculata) under ACTH
control; essential for stress response, metabolism, and homeostasis. Functions:
gluconeogenesis, glycogenolysis, protein catabolism, lipolysis,
anti-inflammatory/immunosuppressive effects, and maintenance of vascular tone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coryza} Acute inflammation of the nasal mucous membranes with nasal discharge and obstruction,
commonly occurring in the common cold or other upper-respiratory infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cosmetic Dermatology} Cosmetic dermatology encompasses aesthetic procedures. Botulinum toxin injections
(Botox, Dysport, Xeomin) relax facial muscles reducing wrinkles. Dermal fillers
(hyaluronic acid, calcium hydroxylapatite, poly-L-lactic acid, polymethylmethacrylate)
restore volume.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cost-Effectiveness} Comparison of costs and outcomes of interventions. Used for resource allocation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Costochondritis} Costochondritis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
chest wall pain. Presents with sharp, reproducible tenderness on palpation of the
anterior chest, usually unilateral involving the 2nd to 5th ribs. May follow respiratory
infection, trauma, or vigorous coughing, but often idiopathic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cot Death} Sudden infant death syndrome, the sudden unexplained death of an apparently healthy
infant, usually during sleep, that remains unexplained after investigation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cough} Cough is a protective reflex that clears the airways; its duration, triggers, sputum, associated symptoms, and examination findings help distinguish infectious, inflammatory, cardiac, and other causes.
foreign material from the tracheobronchial tree.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cough Augmentation} Techniques to improve cough effectiveness in patients with neuromuscular disease or
chest wall weakness. Methods include manually assisted cough (abdominal thrust timed
with cough), mechanical insufflation-exsufflation (CoughAssist device), and breath
stacking with a resuscitation bag.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Count Rate} Number of detected events per second. Dead time losses at high count rates.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Countercurrent Multiplication} The renal medullary process in which opposing tubular fluid flow and selective
permeability along the loop of Henle generate a corticomedullary osmotic gradient. This
gradient permits collecting-duct water reabsorption when antidiuretic hormone is
present.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Countertransference} The therapist's emotional reactions to the patient, which may be influenced by the
therapist's own unresolved conflicts or may be a response to the patient's transference.
Awareness of countertransference is essential for effective therapy and can provide
valuable clinical information about the patient's interpersonal style.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coup-Contrecoup Injury} Brain injury from acceleration-deceleration trauma. Coup: injury at site of impact.
Contrecoup: injury on opposite side. Common in falls, motor vehicle accidents.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Courvoisier Sign} Clinical finding of a palpable non-tender gallbladder in obstructive jaundice suggesting
malignant biliary obstruction. In gallstone disease, the gallbladder is typically
fibrotic and not palpable, whereas in malignant obstruction it is distended because the
wall is initially normal and compliant.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Covered Stent (Vascular)} A vascular stent lined with a graft material that supports the vessel and excludes a leak or
aneurysm from the bloodstream.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Vascular

\medterm{COVID Toe} A chilblain-like skin lesion reported in association with COVID-19, usually affecting
the toes or fingers and appearing red, purple, or swollen.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{COVID-19} Coronavirus disease 2019 caused by SARS-CoV-2, first identified in December 2019. Ranges
from asymptomatic to severe respiratory failure and death. Common symptoms: fever,
cough, dyspnea, loss of taste/smell (anosmia), myalgia, and fatigue. Severe disease:
pneumonia, ARDS, hyperinflammatory state, and thromboembolic complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{COVID-19 in Pregnancy} Pregnant women with COVID-19 are at increased risk of severe illness (ICU admission,
mechanical ventilation, death) compared to non-pregnant women. Risks to the fetus:
preterm birth, cesarean delivery, and possibly stillbirth. Vertical transmission is rare
but possible.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{COVID-19 Test} A test that detects SARS-CoV-2 infection. Nucleic-acid amplification tests, including
RT-PCR, detect viral genetic material, while rapid antigen tests detect viral proteins. Test performance depends on
timing, specimen quality, and the amount of virus present.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cowden Syndrome} Autosomal dominant PTEN hamartoma tumor syndrome (PHTS), caused by loss-of-function
mutations in PTEN (10q23) — a negative regulator of the PI3K/AKT/mTOR pathway. Multiple
hamartomas and increased risk of benign and malignant tumors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cowper's Gland} One of a pair of small male accessory sex glands below the prostate. Its secretion
contributes mucus and fluid to the urethra before ejaculation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cowpox} A zoonotic viral infection caused by cowpox virus or related orthopoxviruses, producing
localized pustular or ulcerative skin lesions and sometimes systemic illness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{COX Inhibitor} A class of drugs that inhibit cyclooxygenase enzymes (COX-1 and COX-2), which catalyze
the conversion of arachidonic acid to prostaglandins and thromboxanes. COX-1 is
constitutively expressed in most tissues, producing prostaglandins that protect the
gastric mucosa, maintain renal blood flow, and support platelet aggregation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coxa Vara} A deformity in which the angle between the femoral neck and shaft is abnormally reduced,
potentially causing a limp, shortened limb, or altered hip mechanics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coxalgia} Pain in the hip or hip joint.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coxiella burnetii} An obligate intracellular bacterium that causes Q fever, a zoonotic infection
transmitted from animals to humans. The organism is transmitted through inhalation of
contaminated aerosols or ingestion of contaminated dairy products. Acute Q fever
presents with fever, headache, and pneumonia. Chronic Q fever can cause endocarditis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coxsackie virus type A16} A member of the Enterovirus genus and the most common cause of hand, foot, and mouth
disease, a mild, self-limited viral illness affecting children. It presents with fever,
oral vesicles and ulcers, and a maculopapular or vesicular rash on the hands and feet.
Complications are rare but may include aseptic meningitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coxsackievirus} A group of enteroviruses that cause a range of human diseases. Group A viruses commonly
cause hand-foot-and-mouth disease and herpangina, whereas group B viruses are associated
with myocarditis, pericarditis, pleurodynia, and aseptic meningitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CPAP} Continuous positive airway pressure delivered through a mask to splint open the upper airway during sleep. It is
commonly used for obstructive sleep apnea and can reduce apneas and related symptoms
when used consistently.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CPAP Machine} Continuous positive airway pressure for sleep apnea.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cracked Tooth Syndrome} Incomplete fracture of tooth causing pain on biting. Diagnosis difficult. Treatment:
crown, root canal if pulpal involvement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crackles (Rales)} Discontinuous, non-musical adventitious lung sounds produced by the sudden explosive
opening of collapsed small airways and alveoli during inspiration or the bubbling of air
through fluid in the bronchioles.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Rales

\medterm{Cradle Cap} A common form of seborrheic dermatitis in infants, causing greasy or flaky scales on the
scalp with mild redness. It is usually harmless and improves with gentle scalp care;
persistent inflammation or signs of infection require assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cranial Arteritis} Giant-cell arteritis, an inflammatory disease of medium and large arteries, especially
branches of the external carotid artery, that can cause headache, visual loss, and
systemic symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cranial Cavity} The hollow space inside the skull that contains and protects the brain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cranial Implant} An artificial device placed in or on the skull or cranial cavity for reconstruction, protection, drainage,
stimulation, or treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cranial Nerve} Twelve paired nerves (CN I-XII) emerging directly from the brain/brainstem, primarily
serving head and neck.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cranial Nerve Palsy} Weakness or paralysis of one or more cranial nerves, causing deficits such as impaired eye
movement, facial weakness, hearing loss, swallowing difficulty, or tongue weakness. The
cause may be vascular, traumatic, compressive, inflammatory, infectious, or metabolic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Craniectomy} Surgical removal of part of the skull without immediate replacement, performed to
relieve intracranial pressure or provide access to the brain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Craniocerebral Injury} An injury involving the scalp, skull, brain, or more than one of these structures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Craniofacial Dysostosis} A congenital disorder involving premature fusion of cranial sutures and characteristic
abnormalities of the face and skull, such as those seen in Crouzon syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Craniopharyngioma} Craniopharyngioma is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cranioplasty} Surgical repair or reconstruction of a skull defect using bone, an implant, or another
suitable material.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Craniosynostosis} Craniosynostosis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations. to the affected suture. Presents with abnormal head shape (scaphocephaly from sagittal fusion, plagiocephaly from coronal fusion). May increase intracranial pressure.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Craniotomy} Surgical opening of the skull (bone flap) for access to intracranial contents.
Indications: brain tumor resection (glioblastoma, meningioma, mets), hematoma evacuation
(SDH, EDH, ICH), aneurysm clipping, AVM resection, and epilepsy surgery (lobectomy).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crazy Paving Pattern} A HRCT finding characterized by ground-glass opacification with superimposed
interlobular septal thickening and intralobular lines. It is seen in pulmonary alveolar
proteinosis, lipoid pneumonia, ARDS, pulmonary hemorrhage, and Pneumocystis pneumonia.
Despite its name, it is not specific to any single diagnosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CRE} Carbapenem-resistant Enterobacteriaceae, resistant to carbapenem antibiotics through
production of carbapenemases including KPC, NDM, and OXA-48-like enzymes. CRE are a
major public health threat due to limited treatment options. Infections are associated
with high mortality rates.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cream} A semisolid emulsion (oil in water or water in oil) for topical application, containing
a drug in an emulsion base. Creams are lighter, less greasy, and easier to spread than
ointments, and are preferred for moist or oozing skin lesions. They penetrate less
deeply than ointments and are cosmetically acceptable.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Creatine Kinase} An enzyme found in muscle, brain, and heart. CK-MB: cardiac isoenzyme (used historically
for MI diagnosis, largely replaced by troponin). CK-MM: skeletal muscle (elevated in
rhabdomyolysis, trauma, statins, myopathy). Total CK: elevated in muscle injury,
myocardial infarction, seizures, hypothyroidism, and statin myopathy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Creatinine} A breakdown product of muscle creatine phosphate produced at a constant rate, filtered
by the glomerulus and minimally reabsorbed; serum creatinine is the most used marker of
kidney function.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CREST Syndrome} A limited cutaneous form of systemic sclerosis characterized by calcinosis, Raynaud phenomenon,
esophageal dysmotility, sclerodactyly, and telangiectasia. It may involve pulmonary
vascular disease and other systemic complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cretinism} An outdated term for severe congenital or early-childhood hypothyroidism causing
impaired physical and intellectual development; modern clinical language uses congenital
hypothyroidism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Creutzfeld-Jakob disease} A rapidly progressive neurodegenerative disease caused by abnormal prion proteins, leading to
cognitive decline, ataxia, myoclonus, and death.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Creutzfeldt Jakob Disease} Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD) is a rare, fatal, transmissible spongiform
encephalopathy caused by the accumulation of misfolded prion protein (PrPSc) in the
brain, leading to rapid, progressive dementia and myoclonus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Creutzfeldt-Jakob Disease} A rapidly progressive fatal neurodegenerative disease caused by abnormal prion protein,
characterized by dementia, myoclonus, ataxia, and other neurologic signs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cri du Chat Syndrome} A congenital genetic disorder caused by deletion of part of the short arm of chromosome
5, characterized in infancy by a high-pitched cry, developmental delay, and
characteristic facial features.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cri-du-Chat Syndrome} A genetic disorder caused by a deletion of the short arm of chromosome 5 (5p-), with the
size of the deletion correlating with severity. Incidence 1 in 15,000-50,000.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cricoid Cartilage} The cricoid cartilage is a complete, ring-shaped cartilage located at the base of the
larynx, directly above the trachea and below the thyroid cartilage, and it is the only
complete cartilaginous ring in the airway, providing structural support and maintaining
airway patency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cricoid Pressure} Cricoid pressure (Sellick maneuver) applies posterior displacement of the cricoid
cartilage against the cervical vertebrae, occluding the esophagus and reducing
aspiration risk during rapid sequence intubation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cricothyrotomy} An emergency airway procedure that creates an opening through the cricothyroid membrane
to provide oxygenation and ventilation when a patient cannot be intubated and cannot be
oxygenated by less invasive methods.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Criminal Abortion} Abortion performed illegally, outside permitted legal indications and procedures (e.g.,
unsafe methods, unqualified practitioners, or non-medical settings), carrying high
maternal risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CRISPR-Cas9} Genome editing tool derived from bacterial immune system. Cas9 enzyme guided by RNA to
specific DNA sequence, creating double-strand break. Repaired by non-homologous end
joining (NHEJ) or homology-directed repair (HDR). Applications: research, therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Critical Care} Specialized care for people with life-threatening illness or injury who need close monitoring and support for failing organs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Critical Findings} Findings requiring immediate communication. Examples: pneumothorax, brain herniation,
aortic dissection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Critical Limb Ischemia} Severe chronic reduction in limb blood flow causing ischemic rest pain, nonhealing wounds, or
gangrene. It requires urgent vascular assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Critical Results} Test results indicating a potentially life-threatening condition that require prompt communication and clinical action.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Critical Value} A laboratory result indicating a potentially life-threatening condition that requires
prompt communication to a responsible clinician or care team. The threshold is test- and
laboratory-specific and should be defined by institutional policy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crohn Disease} Chronic transmural inflammatory condition that can affect any segment of the
gastrointestinal tract from the oral cavity to the perianal region, though the terminal
ileum and ileocolonic region are most commonly involved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crohn Disease Cobblestoning} Endoscopic appearance of the colonic or small bowel mucosa in Crohn disease
characterized by a bumpy, irregular surface resembling cobblestones. This distinctive
appearance results from intersecting lines of deep linear and serpiginous ulceration
that surround areas of relatively preserved or edematous but intact mucosa.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cross-Sectional Study} Measures exposure and outcome at same time. Prevalence study. Cannot establish
temporality.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cross-Tolerance} Tolerance to one substance that extends to another substance with a similar mechanism of
action. For example, tolerance to one benzodiazepine extends to all benzodiazepines, and
tolerance to alcohol extends partially to benzodiazepines due to shared GABA-A receptor
mechanisms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Croup} Croup is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crouzon Syndrome} An autosomal dominant craniosynostosis syndrome caused by mutations in the FGFR2 or
FGFR3 genes, leading to premature fusion of multiple cranial sutures. Features:
brachycephaly (short, broad head from bilateral coronal synostosis), proptosis (shallow
orbits), hypertelorism, midface hypoplasia, beaked nose, and Class III malocclusion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crown (Dental)} Cap covering damaged tooth. Materials: metal, porcelain-fused-to-metal, all-ceramic,
zirconia. Indications: large caries, fractured tooth, root canal treated tooth.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Dental

\medterm{Crown Lengthening} Surgical procedure exposing more tooth structure. Involves gingivectomy and bone
removal. For restorative or aesthetic purposes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crown-Rump Length} The measurement from the top of the fetal head (crown) to the bottom of the buttocks
(rump), the most accurate method for establishing gestational age in the first
trimester. Measured at 7-13 weeks. Accuracy: ±3-5 days. The EDD is set using CRL if the
difference from LMP dating exceeds 7 days. CRL growth: approximately 1 mm per day.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crowning} The stage of the second stage of labor when the fetal head (widest diameter) distends
the perineum and becomes visible at the introitus and is no longer receding between
contractions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CRP} C-reactive protein. Acute phase reactant. Marker of inflammation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crush Syndrome} Systemic manifestations of prolonged muscle crush injury (rhabdomyolysis) following
trauma — typically from collapsed buildings, landslides, entrapment under debris, or
excessive compression of limbs. Bywaters syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crushing Injury} Damage caused when a body part is compressed between heavy objects. It can produce fractures,
nerve and blood-vessel injury, tissue death, and rhabdomyolysis. Severe limb compression
or sudden release after prolonged entrapment requires emergency treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crusted scabies} A severe, highly contagious form of scabies (Norwegian scabies) characterised by thick,
hyperkeratotic crusts teeming with enormous numbers of Sarcoptes scabiei mites,
typically occurring in immunocompromised, elderly, or neurologically impaired
individuals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crutches} Walking aids. Types: axillary, forearm (Loftstrand). Axillary: under arm. Forearm:
through loop.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryoablation} A procedure that freezes and destroys selected tissue, often using imaging to guide the treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryobiopsy} A bronchoscopic technique using rapid freezing to obtain larger lung tissue samples than
conventional transbronchial biopsy. It provides better histopathological specimens for
diagnosing interstitial lung diseases. It requires general anesthesia and bronchoscopic
expertise. It is less invasive than surgical lung biopsy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryoglobulinemic vasculitis} Systemic vasculitis caused by cryoglobulin-containing immune complexes that deposit in
small and medium vessels, classically presenting with purpura, arthralgia, and
glomerulonephritis. Most cases are associated with hepatitis C virus infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryoglobulins} Cryoglobulins is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryopexy} A procedure that uses controlled freezing to destroy or seal abnormal tissue, especially
retinal breaks or small retinal detachments.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryoprecipitate} A plasma fraction rich in fibrinogen, factor VIII, von Willebrand factor, and factor
XIII, used selectively to treat certain bleeding disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryopreservation} Preservation of cells, tissues, or embryos by cooling them to very low temperatures,
usually with protective substances that limit ice-related injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryopyrin-Associated Periodic Syndromes} Autosomal dominant autoinflammatory disorders (NLRP3 mutations, gain-of-function)
causing constitutive inflammasome activation and IL-1beta overproduction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryosurgery} Surgery that destroys abnormal tissue by controlled freezing, used in selected skin
lesions, tumors, and other conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryotherapy} The therapeutic application of extreme cold (typically using liquid nitrogen at $-196^\circ\mathrm{C}$)
to destroy abnormal or diseased tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryptitis} Inflammation of a crypt or small anatomical recess, especially inflammation of the anal
glands and crypts.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryptococcal meningitis} Infection of the meninges by Cryptococcus, usually in people with impaired immunity, causing
headache, fever, altered mental status, or other neurologic symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryptococcosis} Cryptococcosis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
immunocompromised patients (HIV/AIDS, organ transplant, hematologic malignancy).
Pulmonary cryptococcosis: cough, dyspnea, pulmonary nodules. Meningoencephalitis: fever,
headache, altered mental status, and elevated intracranial pressure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryptococcus neoformans} An encapsulated yeast that can cause pulmonary or central nervous system infection, especially in
people with impaired immunity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryptogenic Organizing Pneumonia} An inflammatory lung disease with granulation tissue within alveolar ducts. May be
idiopathic or secondary to infection, drugs, or connective tissue disease. Responds well
to corticosteroids.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryptorchidism} Failure of one or both testes to descend into the scrotum, the most common congenital
genitourinary anomaly in boys (3\% at birth, 1\% at 1 year). 70\% of undescended testes
are palpable (inguinal), 30\% non-palpable (intra-abdominal, absent, or atrophic).
Spontaneous descent by 6 months occurs in about one-third.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryptosporidiosis} A diarrhoeal disease caused by the protozoan parasite Cryptosporidium parvum (zoonotic)
or C. hominis (anthroponotic), transmitted via contaminated water (chlorine-resistant
oocysts), food, or direct contact. Presents with watery diarrhea, abdominal cramps,
nausea, and low-grade fever, lasting 1-2 weeks.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crystal Arthropathy} Joint disease caused by crystal deposition. Gout (monosodium urate) and pseudogout
(calcium pyrophosphate). Diagnosis by joint aspiration and polarized microscopy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crystalloids} Aqueous solutions of mineral salts that distribute between intravascular and
interstitial compartments. Normal saline (0.9 percent NaCl) has 154 mEq/L each of sodium
and chloride, pH 5.4, and is associated with hyperchloremic metabolic acidosis at large
volumes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crystalluria} The presence of crystals in urine, which may reflect precipitation of salts, medication
effects, metabolic disease, or a tendency to form urinary stones.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CSF Analysis} Laboratory examination of cerebrospinal fluid obtained by lumbar puncture. Tests may assess cells,
protein, glucose, microorganisms, and other markers of infection, inflammation, bleeding,
or malignancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CSF Rhinorrhea} Cerebrospinal fluid rhinorrhea is the leakage of cerebrospinal fluid from the
subarachnoid space through a defect in the skull base into the nasal cavity or paranasal
sinuses, resulting in a clear, watery nasal discharge that may be mistaken for allergic
rhinitis or sinusitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CT Angiography} CT imaging with IV contrast timed to visualize arteries. Coronary CTA: assesses coronary
artery stenosis and plaque, calcium scoring (Agatston). CTA chest: pulmonary embolism
(PE protocol), aortic dissection. CTA head/neck: stroke evaluation, aneurysm,
dissection, and vasculitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CT Chest} Detailed lung and mediastinal imaging. High-resolution CT (HRCT) for interstitial lung
disease. CT pulmonary angiography (CTPA) for pulmonary embolism. Lung cancer screening
with low-dose CT.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CT Colonography} CT imaging of the colon after bowel preparation and insufflation with air or carbon
dioxide. Used for colorectal cancer screening in patients unable to undergo optical
colonoscopy and for evaluating complete colonoscopy when the cecum was not reached.
Detects polyps greater than six millimeters with high sensitivity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CT Colonography (Virtual Colonoscopy)} CT imaging of the colon after bowel preparation and air insufflation. Indications:
colorectal cancer screening (alternative to colonoscopy), incomplete colonoscopy due to
obstructing mass, and patients unable to undergo colonoscopy. Sensitivity >90\% for
polyps >10mm. Requires same bowel prep as colonoscopy.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Virtual Colonoscopy

\medterm{CT Contrast} A substance administered during computed tomography to make selected tissues, vessels, or body spaces more visible.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CT Coronary Angiography} CT imaging of coronary arteries after contrast. Detects stenosis, plaque. Alternative to
invasive angiography in low-intermediate risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CT Dose-Length Product} A CT dose metric calculated from the volume CT dose index and scan length. It estimates
the total scanner output for an examination and is used for protocol optimization and
dose monitoring, not as a direct patient-specific risk estimate.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CT Enterography} CT imaging of the small bowel after administration of large volumes of neutral oral
contrast distending the small bowel with IV contrast. Excellent for evaluating Crohn
disease, small bowel tumors, and obscure gastrointestinal bleeding.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CT Perfusion} A CT technique that estimates blood flow and related perfusion parameters in tissue, commonly to assess selected cases of acute stroke or tumors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CT Pulmonary Angiography} CT imaging performed after intravenous contrast to show the pulmonary arteries and evaluate suspected pulmonary embolism or other vascular abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CT Scan} Computed tomography, an imaging method that uses X-rays and computer processing to produce cross-sectional
images of the body.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CT Scan (Computed Tomography)} Cross-sectional imaging using X-rays and computer reconstruction. Non-contrast: evaluate
hemorrhage, stones, and bone. IV contrast (iodinated): evaluate inflammation, infection,
tumor, and vascular structures. Oral contrast: opacify bowel.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Computed Tomography

\medterm{Cubital Tunnel Syndrome} Cubital tunnel syndrome: ulnar nerve compression at elbow (cubital tunnel: Osborne's
ligament, FCU aponeurosis, medial epicondyle, olecranon). Clinical: paresthesias ulnar
1.5 digits, nocturnal, Tinel at elbow, Froment sign (IP flexion of thumb with key
pinch), Wartenberg sign (abduction of small finger), thenar/hypothenar wasting late.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cubitus Valgus} An abnormal outward angulation of the forearm relative to the upper arm when the elbow
is extended.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cubitus Varus} An abnormal inward angulation of the forearm relative to the upper arm when the elbow is
extended, often developing after a childhood elbow fracture.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cuboid} Cube-shaped lateral foot tarsal bone articulating with calcaneus proximally and 4th/5th
metatarsals distally. Attachment for the long plantar ligament and peroneus longus
tendon. Keystone of the lateral longitudinal arch. Cuboid syndrome (subluxation) is a
common lateral foot pain cause in athletes from excessive pronation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Culdocentesis} Surgical puncture of the posterior vaginal fornix to sample or drain fluid from the
rectouterine pouch.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cullen's Sign} Bluish or violaceous periumbilical ecchymosis caused by blood tracking through the
fascial planes to the umbilicus. It is a sign of intra-abdominal or retroperitoneal
haemorrhage, classically severe haemorrhagic pancreatitis, and is associated with
serious underlying disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cultural Psychiatry} The study and clinical practice of how cultural factors influence mental health, illness
expression, diagnosis, and treatment. Cultural concepts of distress, idioms of distress,
and cultural syndromes must be considered. The DSM-5 includes a Cultural Formulation
Interview.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Culture and Sensitivity} Microbiological culture of a clinical specimen (blood, urine, sputum, wound swab, CSF)
to identify pathogens and determine antimicrobial susceptibility. Culture media: blood
agar (most bacteria), MacConkey (Gram-negative), chocolate agar (fastidious: N.
gonorrhoeae, H. influenzae), and selective media.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Culture Media} Nutrient preparations used to grow microorganisms in the laboratory, classified by
composition and purpose.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Culture Swab} Swab for microbial culture. Types: bacterial, viral, fungal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cumulative Dose} Total amount of substance received over time. Important for carcinogens, teratogens,
cumulative toxins.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cumulative Trauma Disorder} A disorder caused by repeated forceful or awkward movements that progressively injure
muscles, tendons, nerves, or other soft tissues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cup-to-Disc Ratio} The cup-to-disc ratio is a clinical measurement used to assess the severity of
glaucomatous optic nerve damage, comparing the diameter of the optic cup (the central
depression within the optic nerve head where neural tissue is absent) to the diameter of
the optic disc (the entire optic nerve head).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CURB-65 Score} One point each for: Confusion, Urea > 7 mmol/L, RR > 30, BP < 90/60, age >= 65. 0--1:
outpatient. 2: consider hospitalization. 3--5: severe.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Curettage} Removal of tissue from a body cavity or surface with a curette, used for diagnosis,
treatment, or removal of abnormal tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Curettage Specimen} Tissue obtained by scraping or scooping a surface or cavity, such as the endometrium or
a bone lesion, for histologic examination. The limited and fragmented nature of the
specimen may restrict assessment of invasion or architecture.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Curette} Spoon-shaped instrument for scraping tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Curling Ulcer} Stress ulceration of the stomach or duodenum associated with severe burns. Part of the
Curling triad with hypovolemia and sepsis. The pathogenesis involves reduced mucosal
blood flow during the systemic inflammatory response to burn injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Curling's Ulcer} An acute peptic ulcer associated with severe burns, often occurring in the duodenum or
stomach during physiologic stress.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Curvularia alcornii} A dematiaceous fungal species causing phaeohyphomycosis, an opportunistic infection of
the skin, sinuses, and occasionally the central nervous system in immunocompromised
patients. It produces dark, septate hyphae in tissue. Diagnosis is by culture and
histopathology showing pigmented fungal elements.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cushing Disease} Cushing syndrome caused by an adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH)-secreting pituitary tumor.
Excess cortisol can cause central weight gain, rounded facial features, skin changes, muscle weakness, high blood
pressure, high blood glucose, and menstrual or mood changes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cushing Syndrome} Clinical state resulting from chronic exposure to excess glucocorticoids regardless of
cause. Most common endogenous cause is Cushing disease from pituitary ACTH-secreting
adenoma accounting for approximately seventy percent.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cushing Syndrome Complications} Untreated Cushing syndrome carries significant morbidity and mortality from
cardiovascular disease, infections, thromboembolic events, and metabolic derangements.
Cardiovascular complications include accelerated atherosclerosis, hypertension, and
dyslipidemia. Infection risk is increased from immunosuppression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cushing Ulcer} Stress ulceration associated with central nervous system disease, trauma, or surgery.
Caused by vagally mediated gastric acid hypersecretion, distinguishing it from Curling
ulcer which results from mucosal ischemia. The ulcers are typically solitary, located in
the stomach or duodenum, and present with gastrointestinal bleeding.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cushing's Disease} Hypercortisolism caused specifically by an adrenocorticotropic-hormone-secreting
pituitary adenoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cushing's Syndrome} The clinical state caused by prolonged excessive exposure to cortisol, from endogenous
overproduction or exogenous corticosteroid use.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cusp Fracture} Breakage of tooth cusp. Often due to large restoration weakening tooth. Treatment: crown
or onlay.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cutaneous diphtheria} A skin infection caused by toxigenic or nontoxigenic Corynebacterium diphtheriae, producing chronic
ulcers or a gray membrane.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cutaneous Drug Eruptions} a spectrum of reactions. Exanthematous (maculopapular) is the most common, appearing 4 to 14 days after drug initiation. Urticarial appears within hours. Photoallergic occurs at sun-exposed sites. Lichenoid resembles lichen planus.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cutaneous larva migrans} A creeping eruption caused by hookworm larvae (Ancylostoma braziliense, A. caninum) from
dog/cat feces penetrating the skin, typically in tropical/subtropical regions (walking
barefoot on contaminated sand). Larvae migrate erratically under the skin causing
intensely pruritic, serpiginous, erythematous tracks.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cutaneous leishmaniasis} A skin infection caused by Leishmania parasites transmitted by sandflies, usually producing one
or more chronic ulcers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cutaneous lupus} A form of lupus erythematosus limited to the skin, manifesting as discoid lesions,
subacute cutaneous plaques, or acute malar erythema. It may occur in isolation or as a
feature of systemic lupus erythematosus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cutaneous Lupus Erythematosus} Autoimmune skin disease associated with lupus-spectrum illness and characterized by
inflammatory lesions that may scar, alter pigmentation, or cause photosensitivity.
Subacute and chronic forms have different morphologies and systemic associations;
evaluation may include biopsy, direct immunofluorescence, and autoimmune testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cutaneous Lymphoma} Cutaneous lymphomas are heterogeneous group of T-cell and B-cell malignancies primarily
involving the skin. Primary cutaneous B-cell lymphomas include marginal zone lymphoma,
follicle center lymphoma, and diffuse large B-cell lymphoma leg type. Primary cutaneous
T-cell lymphomas include mycosis fungoides and Sézary syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cutaneous metastases} Secondary deposits of cancer in the skin, originating from a primary tumor elsewhere in
the body. They may present as nodules, plaques, or ulcers, and often indicate advanced
or recurrent disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cutaneous Small-Vessel Vasculitis} Inflammation and destruction of small dermal blood vessels, commonly presenting as
palpable purpura on dependent areas. Causes include infections, medications, autoimmune
disease, and systemic vasculitis; skin biopsy with appropriate specimen handling is
central to diagnosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cutaneous T-Cell Lymphoma} A malignancy of skin-homing T lymphocytes. Mycosis fungoides is the most common subtype,
presenting as patches, plaques, and tumors in a clothing distribution. Sézary syndrome
is the leukemic variant with erythroderma, lymphadenopathy, and circulating malignant
cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cutting Balloon (Vascular)} A balloon catheter with small blades or scoring elements that create controlled incisions in
plaque during vessel dilation.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Vascular

\medterm{CVA} An abbreviation for cerebrovascular accident, meaning a stroke caused by interruption of blood flow to the brain or
bleeding within the brain. It may cause sudden neurologic deficits.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cyanide Poisoning} Toxicity from cyanide compounds (hydrogen cyanide, potassium/sodium cyanide) that
inhibit cytochrome oxidase (complex IV), blocking oxidative phosphorylation and cellular
respiration despite adequate oxygen—histotoxic hypoxia. Sources: smoke inhalation
(fires—plastic/acrylic), ingestion (industrial, suicidal), occupational exposure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cyanosis} A bluish discoloration of the skin and mucous membranes resulting from excessive amounts
of deoxygenated hemoglobin in the capillary blood. It becomes clinically apparent when
the concentration of deoxygenated hemoglobin exceeds 5 grams per deciliter, which
corresponds to an oxygen saturation of approximately 85 percent.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cephalgia} Headache or pain in the head. It may result from primary headache disorders or from an underlying illness or injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cycle Time} Time for complete image acquisition. PET: minutes. SPECT: 20-30 minutes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cyclothymic Disorder} A chronic mood disorder characterized by numerous periods of hypomanic symptoms and
periods of depressive symptoms over at least two years (one year in children and
adolescents). The hypomanic and depressive symptoms do not meet criteria for a hypomanic
or major depressive episode.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CYP2C19 Polymorphism} CYP2C19 is a cytochrome P450 enzyme involved in the metabolism of approximately 10
percent of drugs, including the antiplatelet agent clopidogrel, proton pump inhibitors,
some antidepressants, and the antiepileptic drug phenytoin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CYP2D6 Polymorphism} CYP2D6 is a cytochrome P450 enzyme responsible for the metabolism of approximately 25
percent of commonly prescribed drugs, including codeine, tamoxifen, many
antidepressants, and antipsychotics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CYP450 System} A family of enzymes, found mainly in the liver, that metabolize many medications and other
foreign substances. Differences in these enzymes and their inhibition or induction can alter drug effects and toxicity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cyst} Cyst is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
gaseous material, and distinguished from an abscess (which contains pus) and a
pseudocyst (which lacks an epithelial lining).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystadenoma} A usually benign glandular epithelial tumor that forms cystic spaces or cyst-like
structures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystic echinococcosis} Hydatid disease: infection with larval cysts of Echinococcus granulosus (dog tapeworm),
transmitted by ingestion of eggs from dog feces. Cysts grow slowly in liver (most
common, 60-70 percent), lungs (20-30 percent), and other organs. Hepatic: RUQ pain,
hepatomegaly, biliary obstruction. Pulmonary: cough, chest pain, hemoptysis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystic Fibrosis} Autosomal recessive disorder caused by CFTR gene mutations (most common: F508).
Defective chloride channel causes thick mucus in lungs, pancreas, liver, reproductive
tract. Symptoms: chronic sinusitis, bronchiectasis, pancreatic insufficiency, male
infertility (absent vas deferens), meconium ileus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystic Hygroma} Congenital lymphatic malformation composed of thin-walled lymphatic cysts, manifesting
as a soft, fluid-filled, painless, translucent (transilluminates brilliantly) mass,
typically in the posterior triangle of the neck (75\%, left-sided), axilla (20\%),
mediastinum, retroperitoneum, or groin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cysticercosis} Cysticercosis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
acquired by ingestion of tapeworm eggs (not by eating pork itself) from contaminated
food, water, or fecal-oral autoinfection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystine and triple phosphate (struvite) crystals} Crystalline substances that may be found in urine sediment. Hexagonal cystine crystals
support cystinuria in the appropriate setting, while struvite crystals may occur in
infection-related alkaline urine; microscopy is interpreted with clinical and laboratory
findings.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
struvite

\medterm{Cystine Stones} Rare stones from inherited cystinuria with defective cystine transport. Hexagonal
crystals on urine microscopy. Difficult to dissolve. Treat with aggressive hydration,
urine alkalinization to pH greater than 7.5, and thiol agents including tiopronin and
D-penicillamine that form soluble complexes with cystine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystinuria} Cystinuria is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
the urine. Cystine stones may form in the urinary tract and cause pain, obstruction, or
recurrent infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystitis} Inflammation of the urinary bladder, typically from bacterial infection, one of the most
common bacterial infections in women.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystocele} Anterior vaginal wall prolapse: herniation of the bladder into the anterior vaginal wall
due to weakening of the pubocervical fascia, most commonly after childbirth and
menopause.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystography} Contrast imaging of bladder. Evaluates trauma, fistulas, vesicoureteral reflux.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystometrogram} Suite of diagnostic procedures designed to evaluate the physiological function of the
lower urinary tract (bladder and urethral sphincter) during filling, storage, and
emptying phases. See urodynamic testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystometry} A urodynamic test that measures bladder pressure and function during filling and emptying.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystoscopy} Cystoscopy is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
cystoscopy: office-based, local anesthesia. Rigid cystoscopy: OR, allows larger
instruments for interventions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystostomy} Surgical creation of an opening from the urinary bladder to the skin or another
structure to permit drainage of urine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystourethrography} Radiographic imaging of the urinary bladder and urethra, usually during filling and
voiding after contrast has been introduced.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytochrome c} Mobile electron carrier between Complex III and IV. Also involved in apoptosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytochrome P450} The cytochrome P450 system is a superfamily of heme-containing monooxygenase enzymes
primarily located in the endoplasmic reticulum of hepatocytes, responsible for Phase I
drug metabolism through oxidation, reduction, and hydrolysis reactions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytogenetics} Study of chromosomes. Detects abnormalities in cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytogenetics (Karyotype)} Visualization of chromosomes under light microscopy from dividing cells (bone marrow,
blood, amniotic fluid, tissue). Staining: G-banding (Giemsa, light and dark bands).
Resolution: 400-550 bands per haploid genome.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Karyotype

\medterm{Cytokine} Small signaling proteins (5-25 kDa) mediating cell-to-cell communication in immunity,
inflammation, and hematopoiesis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytokine Release Syndrome} CRS: systemic inflammatory response from massive cytokine release (IL-6, IFN-gamma, TNF,
IL-10) after T cell engaging therapies (CAR-T, bispecific antibodies, TGN1412).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytokine Storm} A severe, dysregulated systemic inflammatory response involving excessive cytokine
release that can cause tissue injury, organ dysfunction, and shock.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytology} The microscopic study of individual cells, distinct from histology in that tissue
architecture is not examined, used for screening and diagnosis of neoplastic and
infectious disease. Specimens are exfoliative (cervical smears, sputum) or aspirated
(fine needle aspiration, body fluids), interpreted with Papanicolaou or special stains.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytomegalovirus} A herpesvirus that causes significant disease in immunocompromised patients including
transplant recipients and HIV-infected individuals. CMV infects a wide range of cell
types and establishes latent infections in myeloid progenitor cells. Primary infection
is usually asymptomatic in immunocompetent individuals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytomegalovirus Infection} A widespread herpesvirus infection. Primary infection in immunocompetent individuals is
usually asymptomatic or causes mononucleosis-like syndrome. Congenital CMV: most common
congenital infection, causes sensorineural hearing loss, microcephaly, and developmental
delay.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytopathology} The branch of pathology using cytologic examination of cells to diagnose disease,
particularly neoplastic and infectious processes, built on standardized reporting
systems that guide management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytopenia} A reduction in the number of one or more circulating blood-cell lineages: red cells,
white cells, or platelets.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytoreductive Surgery} Surgical removal of as much visible tumor as possible to reduce disease burden, a
cornerstone of ovarian cancer management and combined with hyperthermic intraperitoneal
chemotherapy for peritoneal carcinomatosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytoskeleton} Dynamic protein filament network providing structural support, cell shape, intracellular
transport, and cell division. Three main components: (1) Microfilaments (actin, 7 nm):
cell cortex, stress fibers, contractile ring (cytokinesis), lamellipodia/filopodia
(motility), myosin interaction (muscle contraction).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytotoxic T Cell} The effector cells of cell-mediated immunity that kill virus-infected cells and tumor
cells. Recognize antigenic peptides presented on MHC class I molecules via the T-cell
receptor; co-stimulation by CD4+ helper cells and cytokines (IL-2) is needed for
activation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cytotoxicity} The capacity of an immune effector cell or immune mediator to injure or kill a target
cell. T lymphocytes and natural killer cells commonly use perforin-granzyme pathways or
death-receptor signaling to induce target-cell apoptosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{CADASIL Syndrome} An inherited disorder of small brain arteries that can cause migraine with aura, strokes, mood symptoms, and cognitive decline.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Capnocytophaga Infection} An infection caused by Capnocytophaga bacteria, sometimes transmitted through dog or cat bites or scratches.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Carcinomatosis} Widespread dissemination of carcinoma to multiple surfaces or organs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cardiac Ablation} A procedure that destroys a small area of heart tissue causing an abnormal rhythm.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cavernous Hemangioma} A cluster of enlarged, thin-walled blood vessels that may occur in the skin, brain, or other tissues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cecostomy} A surgically created opening from the cecum to the abdominal wall to allow decompression or stool removal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Central Hypothyroidism} An inadequate thyroid-hormone state caused by insufficient stimulation from the pituitary gland or hypothalamus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chemoradiotherapy} Treatment that combines chemotherapy with radiation therapy, often to improve cancer control.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chlamydial Disease} Infection caused by bacteria of the genus Chlamydia, including sexually transmitted and respiratory infections.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Atrophic Gastritis} Long-term inflammation of the stomach lining that causes loss of gastric glands and may impair vitamin B12 absorption.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Ciliary Dyskinesia} Abnormal movement of cilia that impairs clearance of mucus from the airways and can cause recurrent respiratory infections.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Citrullinemia} An inherited metabolic disorder in which citrulline and ammonia accumulate because of impaired urea-cycle function.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cognitive Behavior Therapy} A structured psychotherapy that identifies and changes unhelpful thoughts and behaviors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Colonic Inertia} Markedly slow movement of contents through the colon, causing severe or persistent constipation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Colonic Stenosis} Narrowing of the colon that may obstruct passage of intestinal contents.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Complement Deficiency} Reduced or absent activity of one or more complement proteins, increasing susceptibility to some infections or autoimmune disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Complete Cystectomy} Surgical removal of the entire urinary bladder.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Coronary Aneurysm} Abnormal widening or ballooning of a coronary artery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crescentic Glomerulonephritis} A rapidly progressive kidney inflammation in which crescent-shaped lesions form in the glomeruli.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cryoglobulinemia} Cryoglobulinemia is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cyclic Neutropenia} Recurrent episodes of abnormally low neutrophil counts occurring at regular intervals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cyclooxygenase Inhibitor} A medicine that reduces production of prostaglandins and may relieve pain and inflammation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Callus} A localized area of thickened, hardened skin caused by repeated pressure or friction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cancellous Bone} Porous, trabecular bone found inside many bones, especially near joints and in the vertebrae.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Capillaries} The smallest blood vessels, where oxygen, nutrients, and waste products move between blood and tissues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Capsaicin} A compound from chili peppers used in some topical medicines to reduce certain types of pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Carbon Monoxide} A colorless, odorless gas that can prevent blood from carrying oxygen and cause life-threatening poisoning.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Carcinogenesis} The process by which normal cells acquire changes that lead to cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cardiac Myocytes} Muscle cells of the heart that generate force for cardiac contraction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cardiopulmonary Bypass} A technique that temporarily circulates and oxygenates blood during some heart operations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cardiovascular} Cardiovascular is a medical term describing the named concept; its precise interpretation depends on its defining features, associated findings, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Carotid Duplex Doppler Scanning} An ultrasound examination that assesses the structure and blood flow of the carotid arteries.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cartilaginous Joint} A joint in which bones are connected by cartilage and allow limited movement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cataplexy} Sudden temporary loss of muscle tone triggered by strong emotion, commonly associated with narcolepsy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Celiac Plexus} A network of nerves in the upper abdomen that supplies abdominal organs and may be targeted for pain treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Pain Syndrome} Persistent pain accompanied by significant physical, emotional, or social effects that interfere with daily life.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cilia} Small hair-like structures on cells that move fluid, particles, or mucus across a tissue surface.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Classic Migraine} A migraine associated with an aura, such as temporary visual or sensory symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Clot Buster} A medicine that dissolves a blood clot; the medical term is thrombolytic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cognitive Reserve} The brain's ability to use alternative strategies or networks to maintain function despite injury or disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Colorectal Adenoma} A gland-forming growth in the colon or rectum that may develop into colorectal cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Complement System} A group of blood proteins that supports immune defense by destroying microbes and promoting inflammation and phagocytosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Compression Fracture} Collapse of a vertebra or other bone because of compression, often related to osteoporosis or trauma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Conception} The beginning of pregnancy, usually referring to fertilization and the subsequent establishment of pregnancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Condyle} A rounded projection at the end of a bone that contributes to a joint.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crohn's Disease} A chronic inflammatory bowel disease that can affect any part of the gastrointestinal tract and often causes abdominal pain and diarrhea.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Crystalline Lens} The transparent flexible structure inside the eye that focuses light onto the retina.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cystoid Macular Edema} Swelling of the macula caused by fluid collecting in small cyst-like spaces within the retina.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{C. Difficile Infection} Infection with Clostridioides difficile bacteria, often causing antibiotic-associated diarrhea and colitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Carcinoma of Unknown Primary} Cancer that has spread but whose original site cannot be identified despite evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cavernous Malformations} Abnormally enlarged, thin-walled blood-vessel clusters that can bleed or cause neurological symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Cavities and Tooth Decay} Progressive destruction of tooth enamel and deeper tooth tissues by acids produced by oral bacteria.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Central Nervous System Lymphoma} A lymphoma that begins in the brain, spinal cord, eye, or cerebrospinal fluid.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Central Nervous System Vascular Malformations} Abnormal blood-vessel formations in or around the brain or spinal cord.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Daily Headaches} Headaches occurring on at least 15 days each month for more than three months.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Chronic Hives} Recurrent itchy welts that occur on most days for more than six weeks.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
